# 02 — Decisions and open questions

Rulings with reasons, newest first, within a day as well as between days. If a ruling is reversed, strike it and add the new one. The tiebreaker is `00-vision.md`. **The parents' logs do not bind here.** A parent's ruling becomes a rule of this game only when it is written in this file.

## Decisions

### 2026-10-15 — A browser host's welcome packed: built, and the multiplayer core is Done

**Before:** a server world gzipped its welcome, a browser host sent the whole world as it was: 2.2 MB for the valley grown whole here, 4.6 MB for a well-explored world, up the host's home connection to the relay and down again for every join and rejoin, and over 16 MB a join would have failed outright (the relay's limit), for browser hosts alone. The browser packs only asynchronously (CompressionStream), and the plan written in TODO (a copy packed between joins, sent while the world had not changed) could not work: the world changes every step.

**Built:** the welcome is the snapshot at the join's tick, as text from the save's cache, **packed in the background while the world steps on**, and sent to that connection when it is done (whole, as a fallback, if the packing fails). The welcome now carries **the tick of its snapshot**. Frames go to every guest at once, and a guest not yet welcomed used to drop them; now **it keeps the frames that reach it before its welcome** (the newest six hundred, thirty seconds of them) and, once the welcome is in, applies those after its tick, in order, before anything newer. **`net.PROTOCOL` is 4,** since a guest of protocol 3 would drop those frames. A server world still packs at once (zlib), its welcome carrying the tick too. `net.status()` names the last welcome's sizes and time (`welcome`) and how many are packing.

**Verified:** the valley grown whole (312 chunks), **2,223 KB to 136 KB**, packed in 15 to 85 ms off the step. The hard case made on purpose in two tabs: the host took a second guest's join inside a step, then opened a door, laid a floor, and sent three frames while the welcome was still packing; the guest, welcomed after them, had the door and the floor (which only those frames carried), and `check("sync")` passed 9 of 9, then 17 of 17 with two guests reporting. A server world through a local relay on protocol 4: welcomed packed with its tick, saved, emptied, and stopped as before. Headless 8 of 8, the hashes unchanged.

**With this the multiplayer core is Done** (Michael's goal, 2026-10-14: capital-D Done, then back to features). What waits is written in `05-someday.md` (spectating, lighter frames on busy nights, the bounded rewind) and in TODO; the `actions/*` versions for the Node 20 deprecation wait for any later commit.

### 2026-10-15 — A save that costs what changed: built (the first of the core's last two moves)

**Measured first, on the valley grown whole and a ring of the wild (312 chunks, 2.1 MB of save):** a save was about 50 ms here, three quarters of it the map's chunks, and **the props are nine tenths of the chunks' bytes** (7,369 of them, the trees most of all), so a cache that left the props out would have saved nothing. **Built:** each chunk's saved form is kept on the chunk, as an object and as text (`game.serialize`, `game.serializeText`, `save.snapshotText`), and made again only once the chunk has changed. Every change that marks a chunk for the wire (`map.touch`, `markDirty`) drops the copy for the chunk and its eight neighbours (a prop is saved with its anchor's chunk, and a change may name another tile of its footprint). **The changes the wire never sends now say so too,** with `map.unsaved`, the anchor's chunk alone: a fire's fuel between whole units, a station's job counting down, a sway's stamp, a door or a board losing health short of breaking, a container's bag and a station's output made on first use. **The rule from now on:** a change to anything on a chunk either marks it (what a guest must see) or calls `map.unsaved` (what only the save keeps); CLAUDE.md says so.

**Proved three ways.** (1) `node tools/savecache.js`, in the headless suite: the valley grown whole, then twenty-four kinds of change through the game's own mutators (a floor, an item down and up, a wall built and destroyed, a prop placed, moved, and removed, a door opened, hit, and boarded, a fire fed and burning, a holder's and a bracket's torch, a sway, a container's bag, a station's job started, counting down, and landing, the wild evicted and grown again, the whole world touched), and after each the cached text, the cached object, and the world made afresh compared character for character; then a change made behind the cache's back, which must be caught. Taking out any one of the new marks fails it. (2) **The cache checks itself in play:** the server's rolling copy (every ten minutes) and its last save on the way down, and the browser's every tenth autosave, make the whole save afresh first (`game.verifySaveCache`) and compare; a chunk the copy had wrong is saved right and said out loud (the server's log: THE SAVE'S CACHE MISSED, with the chunk and the difference; `/status` counts them), so a change that forgets its mark costs at most ten minutes of that chunk and names itself. (3) The pane: `check("save")` 7 of 7, and two tabs on the loopback with a torch mounted, a holder emptied, and a fireplace fed on the host, `check("sync")` 9 of 9.

**The times.** Here: a fresh save 47 ms, the cached text 3, the cached object 2; in the pane the browser's snapshot 18 ms to 1.3. **On the server the gzip (9 ms here) now runs on Node's worker pool,** off the world's thread, and the file is written when it is done; saves finish in the order they were made, one finishing after a newer one was written is dropped, and the last save on the way down is packed at once, so an older save can never land over it. A join's save no longer borrows the welcome's snapshot (the second text of a tick costs a fraction of a millisecond). A crash's state is made afresh, the cache not trusted then. A local server world through a local relay: joins welcomed packed, a save after a leave pausing the world 3 ms, a stop saving and verifying, a rejoin finding the character, a crash writing its own file. **Left as it is:** the browser's store copies the object it is given (about 12 ms for the whole valley here); storing text instead would change every saved world's format, for a pause once a game minute on the host's own machine, and is not worth it now. The first save after a world boots makes every chunk, so it costs what a save always cost.

**Two bugs found by the audit, fixed here.** (1) **A torch mounted in or taken from a holder or a bracket touched nothing,** so a guest saw it only once its chunk went for another reason; `map.setHolder` touches now (the sync run above saw it on the guest). (2) **An authored burner with no fuel of its own held the word "log" as its fuel:** `createProp` read the definition's `fire.fuel`, which names the item it burns, as the count. Feeding it made the fuel not a number: a fire that never went out, its hours unreadable, and its chunk marked for the wire on every step. The fuel is the map's number or none now, and a saved prop holding the word is healed on load. The hashes are unchanged (1cef42ac, and d9a6eb31 with the dev chest); the headless checks 8 of 8.

### 2026-10-15 — Multiplayer's last core work: two moves, then Done; three items wait as later work

Michael: the two moves that finish the multiplayer core are **a save that costs what changed** (each chunk's saved form cached until the chunk changes, for the server world's saves and the browser host's autosave alike) and **a browser host's packed welcome** (the join's whole world gzipped, as a server world already sends it). Each is explained before it is built. With both landed, the core is Done and the work goes back to features. **Waiting, not part of Done:** spectating (the dead player's own body's sight until they choose), lighter frames on busy nights (an animation sent once as a name and a start; an interest radius per guest), and the bounded rewind (a guest's hit judged where things were when the guest saw them, up to 150 ms back). Michael asked that they be written down so they are not forgotten: they are in `05-someday.md`, with what each must be kept possible by, and in TODO.

### 2026-10-14 — The fireball bigger and slower (Michael)

Michael played the fireball: it feels great, but it reads as "a fire baseball" when he wanted a fire bowling ball, and it "absolutely ZIPS" to its target, too strong for what it does. **Bigger:** the fireball's sheet carries `scale: 2` (`archery.fireballSheet`) and the projectile's draw in `render/entities.js` honours a whole-number scale, so the ball is drawn at twice its painted size. A whole number keeps its pixels square, but they are twice the world's pixel size, so it is chunkier than everything round it. The clean fix, if that shows, is the design agent repainting the sheet at the size wanted (cells of 128) and the scale set back to 1. **Slower:** the spell's speed is 5 tiles a second, not 12 (`book.spell.speed` in `data/items.json`), so a ball across its full range of 18 tiles flies about 3.6 s and one at six tiles about 1.2 s. A hit is decided where it lands, as an arrow's is, so a slow ball at a moving target can now miss: that is the balance. Nothing else changes: the damage, the draw, and the flat flight are as they were. **The guest's side:** the speed is the host's data, and the scale lives on the sheet each client builds at boot, so a guest's ball is as big and as slow. Tried: a cast with real mouse events in a lone world, the flight timed at speed 5, and the ball caught mid-flight on screen at its new size, head forward and flames trailing. Lone checks unchanged (determinism d9a6eb31 with the dev chest on, headless the same with `--devchest`; poses 7 of 7), headless 7 of 7. A note for comparing: d9a6eb31 is the world with the dev chest, the pane's default; plain `node tools/world.js` prints 1cef42ac, the same before this change as after.

### 2026-10-15 — Item 5 live: the first real session on a server world, measured; the Fly action pinned

Step 7 merged and deployed (`a24941a`): the relay reported its build, the machine took the volume (the deploy replaced the old machine itself, so the feared hand fix was not needed), `CREATOR_KEY` deployed, the site's `src/build.js` stamped with the same commit. **Michael made a world in his browser, moved it to the server, and played it there.** Read from Fly's log and the machine: he joined, set the world's rules three times, and left; **a minute after the last connection Fly stopped the idle machine**, the relay asked the world to save and stop (SIGTERM), the world saved and exited cleanly, and the machine went down; **a join woke it again**: the machine up and the relay listening in about a second and a half (Fly's proxy refused the first knock for that moment and the guest's socket waited), the world started, and Michael was in within the next second. **Measured on Fly:** a save of this young world 43 to 76 ms (here it was about 20; a well-explored valley's 135 ms here will be two to three times that there, so the owed per-chunk cache matters on Fly); the relay 68 MB resident, one world 97 MB, the machine 108 MB used of 459; the volume 228 KB used of 1 GB. **The costs:** the machine runs only while someone is connected and a minute after, and the volume's size is its own; the money and the traffic are on Fly's dashboard (the CLI does not report them). **The coder agent's note:** the Fly action is pinned to a commit (`setup-flyctl` at the release tag 1.6's, the same commit `master` was on), since that step runs beside the deploy token; bumping the `actions/*` versions for the Node 20 deprecation can wait.

### 2026-10-14 — The fireball: a staff and a spellbook cast it (Michael)

Michael, before the cast's animation: the staff and the book were the only weapons that did nothing. **His rules:** to cast you hold the right element's book (only the Book of Fire, only the fireball) and a magic staff (any, for now); either alone refuses; no choice of spell yet, left click to cast and release to send; the bow's mechanic without the arc; the fireball's drawn picture along the flat path, spun if drawn for it (it is drawn by direction with flicker frames, not to spin), gone the moment it arrives. **Built:** `archery.spellOf` (a staff and a book with a `spell`, in either hand, a launcher of the kind `spell` on the primary button with no ammo; one without the other a launcher that refuses with a word and lets the left button do nothing else), a flat flight (`flat`: the same height all the way, 26 px, so walls, doors, and trunks stop it), `vanish` (nothing lands or lodges), the fireball sheet from the design agent's PNG (`archery.fireballSheet`, its rows mapped onto the held angles, its flicker drawn by the renderer), and a moving light under it (`archery.projectileLights`, on the host and from the frame's list on a guest). The spell's numbers are the book's: `book.spell` in `data/items.json` [damage 70 at a full draw, range 18, speed 12, draw 0.6 s, push 0.4, always staggers and interrupts], `staff: true` on the staff. **Placeholders until `32` item 3:** the draw holds the swing's raised frame and the release its forward one. **Not in this, open in `24`:** a cost beyond the draw and the recovery, an area burst, the landing burst's particles, a choice of spell. **The guest's side:** a guest's cast is its draw and release, resolved on the host as the bow's always has been; the fireball reaches its screen in the frame's arrows, drawn and lit there. Tried: in a lone world with real mouse events, a cast at a zombie six tiles off took it from 100 to 30, the ball flat at 26 px and lit the whole way, nothing left on the ground or in the body, staff and book still in hand; the staff alone refused with its word, no draw and no punch; the ball seen in flight, head forward and the flames trailing; in two tabs a guest's cast flew on the host and was drawn and lit on the guest. Lone checks unchanged (determinism d9a6eb31, players 11, save 7, poses 7, wear 4, craft 9, sleep 9), headless 7 of 7.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 7 built: the deploy (the volume, the image, the workflow, the build id), its first run owed at the merge

Michael allowed the volume and the deploy token. **Made on Fly (the current account, app `cmz-relay`, organisation `cozy-medieval`):** the volume `cmz_data`, 1 GB in `ord`, encrypted, with Fly's scheduled snapshots kept **seven days** (Fly's default is five), a second layer behind each world's own rolling and daily copies; the secret `CREATOR_KEY`, a long random key, **staged** so it takes at the next deploy without restarting the relay that runs today; a **deploy token** scoped to `cmz-relay`, a year long. Both secrets are in a note for Michael, never in the chat or a commit. **The GitHub account this machine's `gh` is signed in as (`writeplaintext`) has no admin on the repository, so Michael adds the token himself** as the repository secret `FLY_API_TOKEN`.

**The image** (`Dockerfile`, `.dockerignore`): `server/`, `src/`, and `data/`'s JSON on `node:22-alpine`, no pictures, fonts, docs, or screenshots; the tree is 3.2 MB and the image 54 MB, built on Fly's remote builder with nothing released; a world and the relay booted from the tree alone, with no picture in it. **`fly.toml`:** the volume at `/data` (`DATA_DIR`), 512 MB (the relay, three worlds, one upload's check), `kill_signal` SIGTERM and `kill_timeout` 30 s for the worlds' saves on the way down; the app's name lives here and in the workflow alone, so the move to the game's own accounts changes those two files and the secrets.

**The workflow** (`.github/workflows/deploy.yml`): every push to `main` deploys the relay and its worlds to Fly with the commit as its build id (`flyctl deploy --remote-only --build-arg BUILD_ID=<sha>`), skipping with a warning while `FLY_API_TOKEN` is not set. **The build id** (the coder agent's review of the plan): the image is stamped (`CMZ_BUILD_ID`), the page carries `src/build.js` (`"dev"` in the repository), the join says it, and **a server world refuses a page of another build with "The game was updated. Reload the page"**, comparing only when both are stamped, so local tests and an unstamped site lean on `PROTOCOL` alone; the relay's front page names its build, which is how a deploy is seen to have landed. **How the site gets its stamp, proposed:** Pages deploys from the `main` branch as it is (the "legacy" source), which cannot stamp a file without a commit of its own; the workflow's second job stamps `src/build.js` with the same commit and deploys the site through GitHub Actions, and runs once Michael switches Pages to deploy from Actions (Settings, Pages, Source) and sets the repository variable `PAGES_FROM_ACTIONS` to `true`. Until then the site deploys as before, unstamped.

**Verified:** against a local server world stamped `serverbuild`, a join from `oldbuild` was told "updated", and the same build, `dev`, and none were taken in; a browser host's join and `check("sync")` 9 of 9; worlds 10 of 10; the headless checks 7 of 7; the hashes d9a6eb31 and 9c26c0df; `fly config validate` passing. **Not yet, and owed at the merge:** the first deploy itself, which the workflow makes on the push to `main` once the secret is in. The relay's one machine was made before the volume, and Fly may refuse to fit a volume to it in place; if the first run fails so, the fix is once, by hand (`fly machine destroy` on the old, stopped machine, then the run again), with the relay away only between the two. After the first real session: the machine's hours, the volume's size, and the traffic, reported.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5's step 6 merged and reviewed: the owner's powers sound, step 5's review closed

Read whole and run before the merge (with the coder agent's own merge of the dead player as a body, `7bf2511`). The owner's character is filed under a fixed `owner` entry that only a join carrying a key whose SHA-256 the meta keeps may take, so a key reissued with the creator key finds the same character; a browser's own hash is a hex digest and can never be `owner`. The owner's endpoints are opened by the owner key alone, a wrong key counted against the address; a new code moves the room and the world's name for it with whoever is in; a removal lets the player go with its reason and bars its browser's hash, read at each join; the rules are clean booleans of the five. Step 5's three points are closed: a map name is a plain name of a map the server has, at the upload and in the world process; a confirm after the wait is refused and a join after the wait drops the old token; the Origin comment says the keys are the locks. Run here against a local relay: a world uploaded with the creator key; a stranger with the code alone was given a new character of its own, and a second device with the owner key took back the owner's character; neither key appeared in the relay's log. Headless 7 of 7, both hashes unchanged. Nothing owed. Step 7, Fly, may begin.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 6 built: the owner's powers, and the review of step 5 closed

**The owner's character is filed under a fixed entry, "owner"**, not under a hash of the key: the owner key proves who may take it and names nothing, so a new key issued for a lost one finds the same character. (Step 5 had filed it under the key's hash, which a new key would have stranded; nothing was deployed, so it changed now.)

**The powers**, endpoints the owner key opens (the relay checks it in constant time each call; a running world is told over the fork's channel, an asleep one only through its files): **the owner's screen** (`/worlds/owner`: the world, its players by name, the kept-out, its rules), opened from the pause menu in a server world of mine or from its row; **a new join code** (`/worlds/rotate`: the old one stops at once, the room moves under the new one and whoever is in stays; this browser's record and a moved world's marker follow); **the world's rules** (`/worlds/rules`: the five, kept in the meta and read over the save's, reaching every guest's frame at once; a guest's own Settings show them read-only as before); **a player removed** (`/worlds/remove`: told "The world's owner removed you from it", let go from the wire, their character dormant, and their browser kept out until the owner lets it back, `/worlds/unbar`; never the owner). **Removing offers a new code in the same step**, since a bar by browser id is undone by clearing the browser's storage and the code is the real lock; the owner's screen says so. **A world of mine on another device**: the Worlds screen's "A world of mine on the server" takes its code and owner key, checks them with the server, and lists it under "My worlds on the server" with Play and Owner. **A lost owner key**: the same screen, with the creator key, has the server issue a new one (`/worlds/rekey`), and the old one stops working.

**The review of step 5:** (1) **no path from an upload**: a save's `mapName` must be a plain name (`^[a-z][a-z0-9_-]{0,31}$`) of a map the server has, at the upload and again in the world process before it reads it. (2) **an expired return cannot confirm**: a confirm after the wait is refused ("the wait ran out"); a join taken after the wait clears the leaving, so its token is dead; and the browser treats a refused confirm as a fresh start, its copy back to a marker and the world asked for again once, where Finish had retried the dead token for ever. (3) **the Origin comment corrected**: the Origin check keeps other sites' pages from using the endpoints through a visitor's browser, and is no lock, since a script can send any Origin; the keys are the locks. **And one found on the way:** the relay closes a guest's socket right after passing on the host's goodbye, and the close could reach the page before the goodbye was read, so a device replaced by the owner's other device was told its line dropped; a goodbye waiting in the inbox now gives its own reason (replaced, removed, refused, full).

**Verified** with the relay on 8013 (`DATA_DIR`, `CREATOR_KEY`, the return's wait at three seconds for the check) and `?nosave` tabs: a map name that is a path, an upper-case one, one the server lacks, and none all refused at the upload, and a save naming a path refused by the world process; device A moved a world up and played it as owner where it stood, its pause menu offering Owner and the owner's screen filled from the server; device B added the world by its code and key (a wrong key refused), found it under "My worlds on the server", and took the same character at the same spot while A was let go and told it was replaced (after the goodbye fix); a friend joined; the owner's rule change reached the friend within a second; the owner removed the friend and changed the code in the same move: the friend was told so, the owner stayed in under the new code, removing the owner was refused, the friend's old code found nothing and the new one said "removed", and let back in the friend came back as the same character; the world's own sync check 17 of 17 with the owner and the friend; the creator key issued a new owner key (a wrong creator key refused), the old key opened nothing and could not add the world, and the new one took the owner's same character; a confirm after the wait refused, a join after the wait taken and the old token dead, and a browser holding a dead token started afresh and brought the world home, the owner's character back under its own id; no key in the relay's log. Alone: the hashes d9a6eb31 and 9c26c0df, the roundtrip, the headless checks 7 of 7, determinism, save 7, wear 4, worlds 10; players 8 of 10 in the hidden pane, its two drawn-and-seen rows needing drawn frames as before. **Left of item 5:** step 7, the deploy (the volume, the image, the GitHub workflow, the build id on both sides).

### 2026-10-14 — A dead player lies as a body you can search (Michael's playtest)

Michael died while away, came back as a new character in the same world, and found his old self standing, not to be searched. **Ruled (Michael):** a player who dies falls and lies as a body, lootable like the dead's, so the next character can get their old things back. **Built:** at the death the world stops whatever the player was doing (a sleep, an action, a draw, a climb), sets down a carried prop beside them where there is room, and plays the fall; once it is over the body is baked like the dead's (`dressCorpse`) and joins the fallen (`S.corpses`), searched with E or the menu like any body (the sword, the clothes, the belt, the bag with what was in it), saved with the corpses, and sent to guests as the dead's bodies are. The client whose player it was keeps it as its own for the dead screen; playing a world whose character of mine has fallen shows the dead screen over my body, never someone else's character (the old fallback to the first player is not reached); the Worlds row reads a body as dead; a rejoin by that browser finds the body and asks for a new character. **The guest's side, found on the way:** a guest who died never saw the dead screen at all (only the host's own death raised it), and its own prediction walked its body back to the idle, a frame a baked body has not got, so the body vanished on its screen: the dead are not moved by the prediction now, the guest's dead screen comes from the record, and its New character rejoins the host with the identity chosen, which makes the new character on the host. Tried alone (a save, leaving before choosing, the Worlds row dead, playing it again over the body, a new character searching the old body for everything it had) and in two tabs (a guest's death: the body on both screens, the dead screen on the guest's, its new character from the host, the old body still there; `check("sync")` 9 of 9). `check("players")` gains the row (11 of 11); determinism d9a6eb31 and the headless checks unchanged. **Not in this:** turning into one of the dead (`31/03`, not built).

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5's step 5 merged and reviewed: the round trip works; two holes for step 6

Read whole and run before the merge, with a local relay holding a creator key and a browser world on a test origin. **The round trip:** moved up with the creator key; the browser's own copy refused to play; joined on the server as its owner, the character where it stood, walked; brought home in two phases; played in the browser at the spot the walk ended. **Two holes, both reproduced:** (1) **a path from an upload**: the save's `mapName` reaches `boot.map`, which reads `data/maps/<mapName>.json`, so an upload naming `../../server/../data/maps/valley` was accepted (201) and every later wake reads the same; only a holder of the creator key can do it, but the plan's own rule is that no path is built from what a client sends; check `mapName` against the maps the server has (a plain name, `^[a-z][a-z0-9_-]{0,31}$`, and the file existing) at the upload and in `server/world.js`. (2) **an expired return can still be confirmed**: once the ten-minute wait runs out the world is joinable again (checked: `/open` says open), so friends may play it, yet the old token still confirmed (200) after the wait was expired on disk, and the server let the world go; the browser would keep the older copy and the friends' play would sit in the trash. Refuse a confirm after `until`, clear `leaving` when a join is taken after it, and on the browser treat a refused confirm as the start again (drop `arriving` and ask for the world anew), where today Finish would retry the dead token for ever. **A note:** the upload's Origin check stops other websites' pages, not a script, which can send any Origin; the creator key is the lock, as the plan says, and the comment claiming scripts are refused should say so. Headless 7 of 7, the hash unchanged.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 5 built: a world moved to the server and brought home, with the owner key

**Up.** On the Worlds screen a browser world of mine, not the one in play, offers **Move to the server**. The first time, the browser asks for the creator key (Michael's to hand out; kept in this browser) on its own screen; then a word of what the move means; then the world's save goes gzipped by HTTPS to the relay (`POST /worlds`, `x-creator-key`). The relay answers **only the game's own site and localhost** (CORS; `CMZ_SITE_ORIGIN`, the Pages site by default; a script with no Origin is refused too), limits tries per address (every try counted, a refused one too: a guessed creator key costs a try), compares the key in constant time, refuses past the stored cap (`CMZ_MAX_WORLDS`, five) and when the running cap is full (the check counts against it), **caps the body compressed at 4 MB before a byte is parsed and again at 64 MB unpacked** (a zip bomb stops there), checks the shape, and then **restores it in a world process** (`--make-only`) before it accepts it; a save that will not restore is turned away and its staging folder removed. The world's id, its code, and its **owner key** (24 random bytes) are the server's own, from the crypto source; the disk keeps only the hashes of the code and the key. **The owner's character is refiled** from the uploading browser's id to the owner key's hash (`k` and its first thirty-two hex), so it follows the key to any device (Michael's default, agreed). The browser keeps the owner key (`identities.owned`), marks its own copy as **on the server** (`home`), which no longer plays here so the world can never fork, and shows the key once, with the code, to copy and keep. That row's **Play** joins the server world with the owner key (`net.setOwnerKey`): the world process files a join that says its key under the key's hash (`hooks.ownerHash`, read from `meta.json` at each join, compared in constant time, never logged), and the owner comes back as their own character.

**Home, in two phases.** **Bring it home** on that row: the relay checks the owner key (`POST /worlds/home`, `x-owner-key`), asks the world's process to save and stop, marks the world as leaving (joins are told it is on its way home), and sends the save with a one-time token. The browser refiles the owner's character back under its own browser id, writes the world as a browser world still marked arriving, and says so (`POST /worlds/home/confirm` with the token); **only then does the server let go**, to a trash folder kept seven days. A broken connection between the phases leaves the row as "on its way home" with **Finish bringing it home**, which sends the word again; if no word comes in ten minutes the world is the server's again. A world can come home with every character dormant, so the client's restore now starts the camera at the spawn when nobody stands, and a row names my character when it is dormant.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5's step 4 merged and reviewed: the relay as the worlds' keeper, sound; three small notes

Read whole and run end to end before the merge. The relay finds a server world by its code's SHA-256 alone (no path from what a client sends; a folder named otherwise or a meta over 64 KB is not read), starts its process with a secret handed in the environment, holds a waking world's guests and passes the first join on, refuses busy and resting worlds with a word, and asks every world to save and stop before it goes; the welcome goes gzipped from a server world and unpacks in order on the guest (what follows waits), PROTOCOL 3. Run here with a world made by `server/worlds.js` and a browser tab as the guest over a local relay: the Worlds dot said open and asleep; the join woke it and the guest was in, through the packed welcome, in about a second; another host for that code was refused, with no secret and with a made-up one, both while it was awake and after it slept; the guest walked, left, and the world saved, emptied, and exited on schedule. Headless 7 of 7, the hash unchanged. **Notes, none blocking:** (1) saves come in pairs at a leave and at the exit (the leave's own and the emptied world's, a moment apart; the dormancy's and the exit's): on a big world on Fly each is a few hundred milliseconds, so skip a save when one ran within the second and the world has not stepped since. (2) A join costs two whole serialisations, the save and the packed welcome; one snapshot could serve both. (3) A browser-hosted world's welcome is still unpacked (no packer answers at once there); a host could keep a packed copy made in the background between joins. Step 5 may begin.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 4 built: the relay as the worlds' keeper, with the review's four points

**The keeper** (`server/relay.js` with `DATA_DIR` set): the server worlds live one folder each under `DATA_DIR/worlds/<worldId>/`, whose `meta.json` keeps the code's SHA-256, never the code, so a copy of the volume lets no one in. A world is found by its code's hash, so no path is ever built from what a client sends; a folder whose name is not a server-made id, or whose meta is too large or not ours, is not a world. **A guest naming a sleeping world's code starts its process** (`server/world.js`, forked, one per world, a heap limit each, at most `CMZ_MAX_RUNNING` at once, three by default for the 512 MB machine) and waits; its join is kept and passed on the moment the world registers, so a sleeping world is entered in about two seconds. Past the cap the guest is told the server is busy; a world that crashed three times in ten minutes rests, and a joiner is told so (both words in the title's notes). `GET /open` says a server world is open whether or not it is awake (`server`, `awake`). On SIGTERM or SIGINT, a deploy or Fly stopping the idle machine, the relay asks every world over the fork's channel to save and stop (a signal reaches no handler on Windows; the channel is the same everywhere), waits up to twenty seconds, and goes. `CMZ_DEBUG=1` adds `/debug/worlds` and `/debug/stop`, answered to the machine itself alone. `server/worlds.js` makes a world by hand (`new`, `list`), the id and the code from the crypto source, until step 5's upload does it from a browser.

**The review of step 3, folded in:** (1) **the save blocks the loop**, so while players are in, a world saves every five game minutes, and always at a join, a leave, a dormancy, a death, when it empties, and on the way down; each save's milliseconds are in the world's `/status` (`save: last, max, count`). Measured here on a well-explored world (373 chunks, 4.46 MB raw): **about 135 ms a save**, 210 KB on the disk; expect two to three times that on Fly. Owed for later, here and in the browser host's autosave: each chunk's serialised form cached until `map.touch` marks it, so a save costs what changed. (2) **A server world's code is reserved:** only the relay's own child, with the secret the relay handed it at the fork (`CMZ_HOST_SECRET`, said once with its first word, `net.setHostSecret`), may register as that code's host; anyone else is refused, even while the world sleeps, and the secret is compared in constant time and never logged. (3) **The welcome is gzipped** by a server world (`hooks.packWelcome`, zlib) and unpacked by the guest with the browser's own `DecompressionStream`, everything after it held in order until the world it follows is restored: the explored world's 4.46 MB welcome goes as 280 KB, packed in 32 ms. A browser host, which has no packer that answers at once, sends its welcome as before. `net.PROTOCOL` is 3, so a stale page and a new host refuse each other. (4) **A crash never overwrites the good save:** the state the process had goes to `crash-<time>.json.gz` beside it (the newest five kept), `save.json.gz` stays the last good one, and `uncaughtException` and `unhandledRejection` take the same path; the next join boots the last good save.

**Verified** with the relay on 8013 keeping two worlds made by `server/worlds.js` and browser tabs as guests: `/open` said the asleep world open and a wrong code not; a stranger's host registration for the reserved code was refused, with and without a made-up secret, while a browser host's own code opened; Ada's join woke the world (booted and registered within the second, joined in 2.4 s, then 1.9 s with the queued join), her whole join 42 KB where the world is 633 KB unpacked; with the cap at one, a guest naming the other world was told busy, an unknown code nohost; Bram joined, Ada walked, the world's own sync check **17 of 17**; a throw left the good save byte for byte, wrote the crash file (holding Ada at the end of her walk), and the relay counted it; Ada's rejoin woke the world from the last good save; after three crashes a fourth join was told the world rests; Bram left, the world saved, held its spell, exited, and `/open` said it asleep; a relay stop with Bram in after a walk had the world save him dormant exactly there and exit before the relay did, and after a restart he came back at that spot; a guest joined the explored world in about two seconds and unpacked its large welcome cleanly. A browser host over the loopback on protocol 3: the welcome unpacked as before, `check("sync")` 9 of 9. Alone: the hashes d9a6eb31 and 9c26c0df, the roundtrip, the headless checks 7 of 7, determinism, players 9, save 7, wear 4. **Not yet:** the Fly volume, the image, and the deploy are step 7; uploads, owner keys, and code rotation are steps 5 and 6.
### 2026-10-14 — Bags: no drag between grids in a world of one's own, and E that opened a sack instead of picking up (Michael's playtest)

Michael: he could not drag from a bag on the ground into his own; an item thrown from that bag onto the ground could not be picked up with `E`; only the menu's Pick up worked. **Two faults, both found and fixed.** (1) **Every move between grids did nothing alone or as a host**, the shift-click, Tidy, and Rotate with it. Claude's fault, from the joiner's fix of 2026-10-13: main.js chooses the local or the remote panel handler per call, and took a handler the local side lacked to mean "the remote one". The local side had never had `moveInGrid`, `quickMove`, `rotate`, or `tidy`, because the panel did them itself when a handler was missing; the remote ones only send to a host, so on a host or alone the move went nowhere. The local handlers now have all four (the grid's own moves), and the switch never takes the remote one off the wire. (2) **`E` with a sack on the ground and an item beside it on the same tile always meant "Open the sack"** (the first of two actions at the same distance), and the sack was already open, so `E` seemed to do nothing. A loose item now wins a tie with a container on its tile: the first `E` picks it up, the next opens the sack. This runs in the world's step, so a guest's `E` is the same. Tried here with the panel's own pointer events: a drag within the bag, from the sack on the ground into the bag, and back; a shift-click; `E` on the dropped item, then on the sack; and on a guest, a move still sent to the host and the frame bringing it back. `check("players")` gains the row (10 of 10); determinism d9a6eb31 and headless unchanged.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 3 built: the world process, and the review's two gaps closed

**The world process** (`server/world.js`): one server world, booted headless from its save, joining the relay as that world's host over a WebSocket with the same wire (`net.js`, which runs under Node 22 unchanged) and the same host (`host.js`) a browser host runs, with a store of its own (whose character is whose, the dormant records, the world's name) and no player of its own. Everyone in it is a guest. **On while anyone is in, held when it empties** (the loop's held flag: nothing moves, the clock stands, the wire answers so a join wakes it); driven by a plain Node timer through the loop's new `drive`, the same fixed step a browser runs. **Saves:** gzipped (`save.json.gz`, the browser's snapshot shape), every game minute, on every join, leave, dormancy, and death, when the world empties, and on the way down; written to a temporary file and renamed, so a crash mid-write never leaves half a save; a rolling copy at most every ten minutes (three kept) and one a day (seven kept), in the world's own folder, against a bad save of our own making, which a volume snapshot taken after it would keep. **On the way down** (empty for its spell, ten minutes by default; a signal, as a deploy sends; the relay gone): everyone still in the world goes dormant under their hash, then the save, then the exit. **At boot** nobody is connected, so whoever a save has standing in the world (a crash saved them so) goes dormant until they rejoin: the grace that would have put them there lived in the dead process's memory, and counts game time, which stands while the world is held. **The rules** are the world's own (`rules.setSource`), read at boot: a world held from boot never steps, and its first save carried the module's starting values (found testing; fixed before the commit). A debug endpoint (`--debug-port`, 127.0.0.1 alone, only when given: `/status`, `/sync/on`, `/sync`, `/stop`) runs the sync check's rows against the process's own ring, and stops it by the path a signal takes, since Windows cannot send Node one. **The data stage** is `server/boot.js`, shared with `tools/world.js`, whose unchanged hashes prove it is the browser's boot; it reads JSON alone, never an image.

**Measured:** a server world under Node is 81 to 102 MB resident. A well-explored world's save (a player taken over the whole valley and 160 tiles of the wild beyond, 373 chunks) is 4.4 MB raw and **190 KB gzipped**; a fresh one 633 KB and 28 KB. So the upload's cap (step 5) is on the compressed size, with a separate limit on the size unpacked against a zip bomb.

**The review's two gaps:** a bite's words now reach the screen of the player bitten, a guest's or anyone's on a server world (`host.js` on `bitten`, as the spent word goes); a blocked blow's sparks are drawn on every guest's screen too (`net.fxAll`, an `fx` message the guest draws with `burning.spark`, the numbers bounded on arrival). **And one found on the way:** the sync check hashed health to three decimals while the wire carries two, so a bleeding player, whose health drains in fractions every step, failed the players part though nothing differed; it compares what the wire promises now (for the dead too), as it already does a fire's fuel.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 2 built: the world's rules ride in its save (SAVE_VERSION 2, the first migration)

A world's rules (zombie aggro, zombies attacking villagers, survival, free building, multi-hit) were the hosting browser's settings, read again each step; a server world has no settings, so **the rules are the world's now:** `game.serialize` writes them, `game.restore` sets them (a welcome is a serialize, so a guest's rules come with the world before any frame), and the sync check compares them as a ninth part. **Where a world reads them each step** (`rules.setSource`): a browser's own world from its settings as always, so playing it writes the settings into its save; a server world from a source the process names, its own save's rules, which the settings never touch (proved in two tabs by a host reading from a source with survival flipped against its setting: the guest's rules came from the welcome as the host's, and the host's own Settings showed the host's). **The save's version is 2**, and `save.upgrade` is the table of migrations from each version to the next, run when the browser's store or the old slot reads a save (the server's files will run the same): a version-1 save gains the rules from the settings' defaults (`rules.DEFAULTS`); a save with no version or a newer one is refused. The join carries the version, so a page and a host of different versions refuse each other, as before.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5, step 1 built: the host as a module, and the bite as the world's rule (the plan with the coder agent's additions)

**The move.** Everything a world's authority does beyond `game.step` is out of `main.js` and in `src/host.js`, one module the browser host and the server world share: the joins and whose character is whose (`joinFor`, `clampIdentity`, the owners and the dormant records behind a store the client hands in), dormancy, stepping away and back, the host's side of the wire's hooks (a guest's panel command, Use, placement, and Make), and the answers to the world's events for the players they are for (the kick to a guest's camera, the spent word to a guest's screen, the craft line to the guests near, an opened container to the guest who opened it, the save on a death), with the routing of a message or a log line said while a guest's player acts. The client hands in what differs between a browser and a server (`host.init`): the store, the save, its own player (a server has none), and what to do with a character put back (the browser rebakes its sheet). `main.js` keeps what is a screen's and **only reads the world** (the rule after this step, the coder agent's first addition): its camera, its messages, its panels, its own stepping away.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 5 ruled: worlds on a server, for availability (Michael)

**The goal is availability:** a friend can play a shared world while its owner is offline. A server world is **on when anyone is in it** and held when empty, as a host's tab is (the clock law: nothing moves while nobody plays, and it costs nothing asleep). Everyone in a server world is a guest, the owner included, which Michael likes for its parity. **Who runs it:** Fly, beside the relay. **Who pays:** Michael, as playtesting for his own benefit. **Where the saves live:** on the server, for a server world. **When:** now, on the current Fly account (moved to the game's own accounts on the infrastructure day). **Deploys:** automatic from GitHub on every push to `main`, since the protocol refuses a mismatch. **Ownership and access:** the server owns the world; the owner authenticates as owner from any device and controls who gets in; a stranger who finds the page can neither join a world nor make one (details left to the build, reviewed here). **Single player stays local** (Claude's recommendation, Michael asked): the demo is single player on a static site and a Steam game is expected to play offline, so the in-browser path stays regardless, and the sim is the same `game.js` on both; any world, a solo one included, may be moved to the server by its owner, which gives the joiner's view for testing when wanted. Planned before it is built: the multiplayer branch writes the plan, reviewed here, as for items 1 and 3.

### 2026-10-14 — Item 4 built: a guest's own kick and spent word, the host's rules read-only on a guest's Settings, the belt keys and a click on a villager through the host

The fourth of the five items for a friend on their own computer, with the two gaps the docs pass found folded in; all five are a guest's commands or events. **The camera's kick** for a guest's own landed blow: the swing resolves on the host, so the host sends the kick to that guest alone (`net.kickTo`, a `kick` message, the numbers bounded on arrival) and the guest's camera jolts as the host's does. **The "is spent" word** for a guest's weapon worn out on the host goes to the guest's screen and log (`net.messageTo`), never the host's. **The world rules on a guest's Settings** (zombie aggro, zombies attacking villagers, survival, free building, multi-hit; `docs/31/07`, question 13) show the host's values, greyed and marked "(the host's)", and cannot be changed there; the guest's own preferences (the interface, the zoom, the cursor, the aim guide, and the rest) stay its own. **The belt keys** (1 to 4) are key events in the player's snapshot like E, B, and T (`interact.beltEvents`, `updateInteract`), performed by the world's step for the player whose client pressed them, so a guest's bread is eaten on the host and its swap made there; on a guest they had changed only its own replica, which the host's record then put back. They still work with the bag open. **A guest's left click on a villager** names the villager by its id (`net.sendPokeVillager`), since the host has no cursor of the guest's; the host answers within the click's reach (`interact.pokeVillagerFromGuest`), the same poke its own click makes, and the villager's turn and line reach everyone in its record.

### 2026-10-14 — A world held while its host is away with nobody in it, and the Worker's tick slowed when nothing wants it (the coder agent's review of item 3)

**The fault:** a host stepped away when the last guest left kept the world running with nobody in it, since a world open to friends never pauses. **Fixed:** the world is **held** while its host is away with no guest in (`loop.setHeld`, a flag apart from the pause, which a screen and a scripted test own): it does not step and its clock does not run, as a solo world's would not; while held the loop still answers the wire each frame or tick (`hooks.pump`, the host's step with nothing in it), so a friend's join is heard and starts the world again, and the host's own input brings the host back and lets it go. A first version reused the pause and would have unpaused a scripted test's tabs and eaten its queued inputs; caught before it ran. **The note:** the Worker ticked sixty times a second in every tab, a solo one included, where the ticks were only ignored; its pace is now the page's to set, sixty a second while a world wants driving and one a second while nothing does, so a laptop's battery is spared. **Verified in two tabs on the loopback**, the host in a background tab: away with a guest in, the world ran at a second a second; the guest gone, held: no game time, no clock, no fuel burned in two seconds; a friend joining the held world started it at a second a second with the host still away; the host's key brought it back; forced away with nobody in, held, and a key let it go; the wire closed, the Worker set to one tick a second, opened again, to sixty. Michael agreed five rules, written into `36`: (1) **the first run reaches the first night**, where the game looks best (a new player sees nightfall, a careful one dawn); (2) **preparation buys time, never escape** (Michael's correction: the demo still kills everyone, since one you could survive forever is a free game; early deaths read as the player's mistake, each escalation stage answerable for a while, the end certain); (3) **the edge promises the world instead of hiding it**: a bramble thicket the player cannot cross and the dead push through, roads out blocked by a fallen tree and a wrecked cart, signposts naming the valley's places, no invisible wall a player can walk into (the outer boundary only the dead reach); (4) **the end card sells the full game**: the run and the best run, a few lines on what the full game is (pictures once there is final art of it), and **a Wishlist on Steam button**, the card's largest, to the store page (Michael: or the art money may be wasted), also on the demo's title; (5) **the depth shows along the way**: the set laid out so a first day touches boarding, the bench, the hearth, the well, the bag, and wear. ### 2026-10-13 — Item 3 built: a world with friends in it runs on behind a hidden host, and a player idle five minutes steps away (the plan with the coder agent's changes)

**The driver.** The loop steps the world from the browser's drawing frames, which a hidden tab does not get, so a host who switched away froze the world for everyone. A second driver (`loop.setBackgroundDriver`): a Worker posts an empty tick every 16 ms, and once the browser's frames have stopped for a tenth of a second each tick steps the world by the real time since the last, without drawing; a frame drawn again takes over at once, the two sharing the clock and the accumulator so a switch neither doubles nor drops a step. The stall is measured, not read from the visibility flag, which a covered window or an embedded pane may not set (the pane's own background tabs say "visible" and draw nothing). The client answers what a hidden world does: a world with friends in it, or a guest's, steps; **a host open to friends with nobody in it yet only answers the wire** (`pump`: the join is heard, the world does not move and its clock does not run; found while testing the plan: a host who opened and switched to Discord to send the code would otherwise have stranded the friend's join); a solo world does nothing and stays stopped, as ruled. The hidden host's title says "Hosting: friends are playing".

**A silent guest stops.** A guest whose tab hides sends one input with nothing held at once (`net.idleNow`), and the host lets any guest's held keys lapse after half a second without input (`SILENT_STEPS`, times the time scale), so a frozen tab or a dead line no longer walks its character on for ever with the last input (the coder agent's point 3).

**Stepping away** (rulings above). Each client measures its own idle time in real time from its keys, clicks, mouse movement, and wheel; at five minutes in a world with friends in it (a host with guests, or a joined guest), and never asleep, dead, or mid-climb, it steps away: the host's own through `awayNow`, a guest's by an `away` command the host performs. Away is the dormant path without the disconnect: the character leaves the world with its record filed under its browser's hash (so an autosave and a reload keep it, and Play puts it back where it stood), every log says "stepped away", and the client shows "You stepped away" with the world running on behind it. Its next key, click, or mouse movement brings it back (`back`, `backNow`), and that key or click is spent on coming back and goes no further. The host refuses a step away for a sleeper, the dead, or a climb and answers `stay`. An away guest's inputs and commands (input, act, item, use, place, craft, poke) are refused; nothing is sent or predicted while away. **The coder agent's three faults, closed:** `game.removePlayer` no longer hands the client's own pointer to the next player in the list (on a host that was a guest's character, its camera and its input); the client's pointer stays on its own character out of the list, so the ninety readers in `main.js` read a body held where it stood and nothing throws; an autosave while the host is away writes its record as dormant under its own hash, and Play after a reload restores it, not `S.players[0]`; the panels, the Use, placement, and build modes, and the menu close on stepping away.

**Verified in the pane** (a background tab there draws nothing but keeps its timers: the hidden host exactly). A host open with nobody in: no game time in three seconds; a guest joined it with neither tab drawing a frame. With the guest in: the hidden host stepped at 60.2 a second, every step the Worker's, the dead moving, 20 frames a second to the guest, zero correction. The handover, on a copy of the loop with its frames stopped and restarted: 59.7 steps a second drawn (the Worker idle), 59.3 by the Worker when the frames stopped, 60.1 when they came back, never twice. A guest walking whose loop then froze: its character stood still on the host within a quarter of a second; a guest hidden mid-walk sent one idle input and its character was standing at once. A guest's swing on the hidden host: 100 to 66, zero correction. `check("sync")` 8 of 8 in real time with the host hidden. The host stepped away while hidden with a guest's chest open: out of the list, its pointer kept, its record dormant, the world stepping on at 60, steps and a drawn frame with no throw, the guest's chest still open and "stepped away" in its log; a zombie put on its spot never targeted it; a key on the page brought it back at full health, the key spent (no step taken, not held). The guest stepped away: out of the host's list, its record filed by its hash, no input sent, a command while away doing nothing; a key brought it back where it stood, zero correction. A guest in bed did not step away; forced to ask, the host refused and it was back in. `check("sync")` 8 of 8 after all of it. A solo world in a background tab, unpaused: no steps; a solo player idle past the time: still in. A real world hosted with a guest: the host away, an autosave with its record dormant under its hash at the spot it stood, the tab reloaded, Play: back where it stood at full health, picked as this client's player and not the guest's; the test world and identity deleted after, the settings as found. Alone: the hash d9a6eb31, save 7, sleep 9, wear 4, craft 9, players 9, worlds 10, the headless checks 5 of 5. **A testing note:** two tabs of one browser share its browser id, so a test of stepping away or a rejoin gives the guest its own (`net.setClient` before `net.open({ join })`); otherwise the host and the guest are one owner. **Still owed:** Michael's run of `tools/background-probe.html` in his own browsers (hidden, minimised, covered, six minutes each, with the savers' states), and a real session on Pages with the host switched away for ten minutes.
### 2026-10-13 — A sleeping player never steps away (Michael, on item 3's plan)

Item 3's plan steps a player away from a shared world after five minutes of real time with no input (the multiplayer branch's rulings). A player in bed sends no input, and waiting in bed for friends who are still up can pass five minutes; stepped away, they would leave the bed and the count of sleepers, and come back to a world whose night had moved on without them. **Ruled:** a sleeping player is never stepped away. Lying down is a choice already made, and a bite in bed is the danger the design keeps (`30` section 3.3). Also asked of item 3 in review: a guest whose tab hides mid-walk must stop on the host (today the host reuses its last input), and the host's own player out of the world while its client stays open must leave the client's "me" its own (today `removePlayer` hands it to the next player in the list).

### 2026-10-13 — When the world stops, and when a player steps away (Michael, for item 3: the host's world while its tab is not in front)

**Why the host's world stops today:** the loop steps the world from the browser's drawing frames, and every browser stops drawing a hidden tab, so a host who switches tabs or minimises freezes the world for everyone (measured in the pane's background tab: no drawing frames, while a Worker's timer ran at 62 a second). The plan for item 3 drives the host's loop from a Worker's timer while its tab is hidden; Michael asked why the game should ever stop while the tab is still open, and whether a generous idle guard would do instead. **Ruled:**

1. **A solo world stops while its tab is hidden**, as single player always has: switching away is a pause, and nobody is waiting on you.
2. **A world with friends in it never stops while its host's tab is open**, hidden, minimised, or covered (the Worker's timer; tabs a browser freezes outright are the one exception, which the probe of step 0 measures).
3. **In a world with friends in it, a player with no input for five minutes of real time steps away**: the character leaves the world, safe, as a guest's does after its sixty seconds disconnected, and comes back where it stood on the next key, click, or mouse movement. The guard is on the character, not the world: stopping the world after an idle spell would strand friends still playing, and a body standing still for two hours is a gift to the dead (Claude's point, Michael agreeing).
4. **Never in a solo world**: Michael often stands still in his own games for half an hour while doing something else, and wants to stay in.

**Claude's assumption, for Michael to overrule:** rule 3 covers guests as well as the host. A guest whose tab is hidden sends nothing but stays connected, so today it stands in the world for as long as it likes, which is the same gift; the same five minutes apply to everyone in a shared world. The idle time is the player's own client's to measure (its keys and mouse, real time, since it is about the person and not the world), and stepping away and back are commands the host performs, so the world's step stays the only thing that changes the world.

### 2026-10-13 — A guest's own swing played at once, and a sleeping guest's inputs sent as runs (the second of the five items for a friend on their own computer)

**The swing at once** (`31/03`, `31/04`: the animation is cosmetic, the hit is the authority's). A guest's own player now runs the controller in a predict mode after its walking, in the host's own order (`game.step`): the guard raised with the right button, the swing's start on a left click, the halberd's thrust on the middle, the walking speed those set, and the combat clocks ticking. The swing starts exactly as on the host (the gate, the stamina, the hand's clock, the swing lock, the exposure, the animation; `combat.tryAttack`'s `predict`) and stops there: no targets, no damage, no roll of the generator, no hit-stop, no kick, no wear. The pokes, the bow's draw and loose, and the shove stay the host's. It runs on the guest's step and again in the replay over each frame, so when the host's record arrives the replay carries the swing on from the host's truth, and a swing the host refused (the weapon gone, the cooldown not yet out) is undone by the next frame. **Two fixes it needed:** the clocks that gate a swing (`attackCooldown`, `offCooldown`, `swingLock`) go whole in a player's light record, since rounded to two decimals a cooldown of 0.0167 would go as 0.02 and the guest would hold a click the host took; and a frame whose record of my player is the acknowledgement alone now replays from the last record the host sent (kept as it arrived) rather than over the predicted player, which would have applied the unacknowledged inputs twice. **And one found on the way:** a guest's left click that a placement confirm took was still sent to the host as a click, so building a wall also swung there; it no longer is.

**Verified in two tabs on the loopback.** A sword in the guest's hand: the click step itself showed the attack at elapsed 0 and the cooldown at 0.483, with the host not yet stepped; the host took the click and its copy ran the same numbers step for step; when its frames arrived the guest's animation went on from frame 1 at 0.000 to 0.017 with no restart or jump and zero correction. The sword taken away on the host before a second click reached it: the guest showed the swing at once and was back at idle with the cooldown at zero on the first frame after. A zombie in reach and in sight: the host's hit took it from 100 to 66 with the sword's wear, the guest's copy untouched until the frame brought 66. A shield: the guard raised on the guest's own step with the walking speed at 0.45. Two weapons, a click every third step for twenty-seven steps: the main hand, the swing lock, and the off hand once the lock cleared, line for line the same on both sides. `check("sync")` 8 of 8 after the swings.

**The sleeping guest's inputs** (the real-time sleep test the coder agent asked for, run once as play runs it: each tab advanced by the world's time scale per emulated browser frame). The host's frames coalesced as designed, about 37 KB per half real second asleep. But a guest's own loop runs sixty times faster too and sent an input every step: about 530 KB per half real second, roughly a megabyte a second upstream from a guest lying in bed, enough to swamp a home upload and the relay. **Fixed:** while the world runs above its normal speed, the same input step after step is held as a run and sent on the frames' own cadence (a running count of one over the scale reaching three), and the host expands a run back into one input a step, so the acknowledgement and the replay stay one to one; an input that differs, or carries a press or a key event, goes at once, the run before it first; at the normal speed every input goes at once as before, so nothing waits. The host's backlog bound is a sixth of a second of the world's steps, ten times the scale. **Measured:** asleep, ten run messages and about 3 KB per half real second, about 6 KB a second where it was a megabyte; awake, 17.5 KB a second as before. Woken with both on the host, the guest walked and stopped with the two positions equal and zero correction; `check("sync")` 8 of 8 at tick 3721 after the night.

**The coder agent's rule, kept for every delta:** anything the host remembers about what the guests have must be forgotten when the thing it describes leaves the world, and when a guest's baseline is reset (written into `31/06`). Alone: the hash d9a6eb31 in the pane and headless, wear 4 of 4, the headless checks 5 of 5; `check("players")` fails its two drawn-and-seen rows in a hidden pane with and without this change alike (they judge rendered frames).

### 2026-10-13 — The map's choices, and the long arc: normal until it isn't (Michael)

**The map on `M` (for its scope):** (1) it shows what has been seen, by anyone in the world, kept by the world: a new character sees it all, a death wipes nothing (Zomboid may reveal only what you yourself saw; here the world remembers). (2) Player-built walls are shown (they carry `wall.built`; a different ink is allowed). (3) Every player in the world is shown live, with a name; never the dead, never villagers. (4) The world keeps running behind it, alone as with friends: browsing the map is a risk. (5) Minecraft's way: low fidelity, pixel-simplified, and accurate before pretty (when you move, you move on the map). (6) Drawn from the same angle as the screen, as Zomboid's is: map north is the world's north (`-y`, the `n` wall slot), which is up and to the right on screen (`iso.toScreen`), so "map north" means the same to everyone. (7) Pins, notes, a death marker, zoom and pan: someday, wanted.

**The long arc (Michael's picture; two reference images, `docs/images/2026-10-13-vibe-start.webp` and `-vibe-end.webp`):** everything is normal until it is not. A new world starts with no dead anywhere and a whole first day with no danger, villagers talking you through the basics (farming, cooking, healing, weapons). Then the dead come, and the survival game begins (one day the town may be restored while you hold them off; for now you survive). Exploring, or prompted by villagers, you meet spookier things, fight bosses, open new places, and by the end the dead are a nuisance because you have fought something like a demon lord. No story by design (the villagers may tell lore), no end boss, no win screen. What keeps it from being an RPG is the survival half: crops to bring in, supplies running out, weapons spent, so you go home (or later build a forward base). Why go out: the challenge, and things in the far places you badly want at home (Minecraft's End for the Elytra, the Nether for potions): **the more the regions and the survival and crafting systems depend on one another, the better.** The picture people will talk about: a severed demon head brought home and hung in the cosy valley where you started.

**Open, Claude's notes for Michael:** (a) the dead's arrival on day one is a scheduled event, the one place the arc touches the clock law; it is a premise, not a recurring escalation, but it can be made chosen (the dead come with the first nightfall or the first sleep, or a world option skips the quiet day); (b) every region, before it is built, answers what it gives that home wants (the Elytra test); (c) the first picture is brighter and greener than `06`'s dull grass and dusky shade, so the starting register may want revisiting; the shift toward the second is most cheaply palette and light per zone, which the engine already carries; (d) where 1.0 ends (the valley alone, or the valley with one frontier and one boss as proof of the arc) is undecided.

### 2026-10-13 — What this game is: a survival sandbox with a frontier; never harder on a clock; skills off the battlefield; talk that never gates; bosses as gates (Michael)

From a conversation on the game's identity (the Zomboid against Stardew tension since day one), before scoping the map and scripted sequences. **The frame (Claude's proposal, Michael's agreement):** home is Stardew-shaped (the farm, the hearth, the villagers, the reward loop), the frontier is Zomboid-shaped (serious and lethal), and the arc is geography, not story: things get darker the farther and deeper you go, and you choose when to push. The world remembers progress (the cottage, the fields, a cleared place, an opened gate); the character is mortal and takes its skills and what it carries with it. Valheim is the nearest model (a survival game whose biomes are gated by bosses, and a great co-op game partly for that reason).

**Never harder on a clock (Michael, a law, in `00`):** no world decay, no shutoffs, no helicopter, no horde night, no population that grows with the days. Every rise in danger is one a player chose ("I went to the bog and accidentally brought some bog danger home with me"), even when danger at home rises with it. The reason: a co-op pair where one plays forty hours a week and the other two; Zomboid and 7 Days to Die punish the one who plays on alone, so he stops playing to wait. The game already passes: needs drain only while a character is in the world (a guest goes dormant after sixty seconds away), crops and fires move only while someone plays, which is a gift to the one who returns, and night cycles without escalating. **To hold when built:** the trickle of `03` refills toward fixed caps answering noise and light, never grows with days survived. **Open (Claude's note, not ruled):** in a shared world one player's choice escalates danger for an absent friend; the cleanest form may be escalation that stays where it was brought (the dead that followed you, a curse riding a character) rather than a world-wide level.

**Skills are off the battlefield (Michael):** no skill makes you hit harder. One per station family at most: cooking, farming, healing, smithing, and one for cloth and leather; for 1.0, if any, only those that touch non-combat things already in the game. Death takes them with the character. Claude's test, agreed in spirit: a skill shows best as things you make (a friend eats your stew, wears your leather), which turns one player's hours into gifts rather than a gap. **Healing** is a skill and a station: several healing items with trade-offs, and the zombie cures made there as soon as the ingredients are gathered, not gated by skill.

**Recipes: law 1 of `30` restated (Michael):** you know every recipe you need to play effectively from the start. Skill may unlock more, but those unlocks are flavour (a fun dish because it is fun), not power; depth and power grow only as far as the rest of the game asks for them (a hard boss could justify stronger food; until such things exist, nothing does). Replaces "all known from the start" as the whole rule.

**Talk never gates (Michael):** dialogue trees are in, as exposition and personality, the OSRS register and not forgettable fetch quests; chatting up villagers is how you learn the world. No dialogue is load-bearing and none changes world state. Talk may read the world and change with it (once the dead arrive in a world that began without them, the townsfolk speak of them), but never write it. Claude's note: this also makes it cheap for multiplayer, a conversation being a panel on the talker's screen with nothing to sync or save. **A scope is owed** (with the map and scripted sequences). Supersedes `04`'s "no quests, no dialogue trees" and the "not an RPG" line of `00` as far as talk goes.

**Bosses gate regions (Michael, as general rules for later):** when the player outgrows the dead, the next region opens behind a deliberately hard boss, the place to prove skill with the weapons and their trade-offs. Two rules with it (Claude's, accepted): the region behind the gate is dangerous for the gear of its time, not only the boss; and a boss can be fought again, so the gate opens once for the world but a friend who missed the fight can still have it. Far off; nothing scheduled.

**Owed before any boss or skills scope:** a session played as the game is meant to be (combat, aggro, the valley, the dead round the spawn; the combat-testing dead are still placed for the proving ground, `TODO.md`), since difficulty has not been felt yet.
