### 2026-10-13 — Multiplayer is live: every addition and every change is built for it in the same commit (Michael)

Michael: now that friends play over the relay, every new addition or change must account for multiplayer; it must not lag a week behind and leave a pile of catch-up. **Ruled:** a change that works for the host alone is not done. It runs in the authority's step, a guest reaches it through the same verb, handler, key event, or command as the host's player, its changes travel in the save (the welcome) and the frame, its world state is in the sync check, and it is tried with a guest in two tabs before it is called done. This replaces the co-op tiebreaker of 2026-10-12 ("all else equal, the multiplayer-friendly fork; single player first"), which only broke ties. Written in four places so no session misses it: `CLAUDE.md` (start-here item 6 and the first of the rules, with the checklist), `TODO.md` (above the Status block), `docs/31-multiplayer/08` (now the rules every change follows, the old six habits kept as the floor), and `05-someday.md`'s co-op entry (no longer someday).

### 2026-10-13 — The wire made to fit a home connection, merged and reviewed: two faults in the delta and one promise not kept

The five wire commits (the dead as a delta with a roster, a player's record in a light and a heavy part, frames every third step paced by a running count, the replica smoothed between frames, the sync check's marked frames) merged with no conflict; the pacing by the world's own time over its time scale (not the wall clock) and the running count are right, and keep the scripted tests whole. **Found reviewing, fixed here:** (1) **A character that left the world and came back was never sent again to the guests.** The host remembered what it last sent of each character, and when a far chunk is put to sleep its dead leave the lists and wake later with the same id and the same record: the record looked unchanged, the roster named an id the guest had dropped, and the zombie stood on the host, hunting, and nowhere on the guest's screen. Reproduced in three tabs (host 77, guests 76), fixed: an id that is no longer in any list is forgotten (`known`, `lastSent`, and a player's last heavy and light parts too, since a player gone dormant and back had the same hole), and both guests then showed 77. (2) **A second guest's baseline.** The welcome is a new guest's baseline, but what the host remembered was the first guest's; a record that changed and changed back between the last frame and the welcome would never be corrected for the newcomer. Every welcome now makes the host forget what it sent (`forgetSent`), so the next frame carries every record, roster, and extra again. (3) **A promise not kept:** the reply said the sync check's dead part now covered facing, animation, line, and burning; the diff did not carry it. It does now (facing, animation name and frame, burning; the line expires on each client's own clock). **And a waste found measuring:** each animation's timer (`elapsed`) ticks every step, so every one of the dead near the living, standing still or not, counted as changed in every frame; it is rounded and left out of the change test (the guest draws by the frame number). **Measured** (three tabs on the loopback, 22 of the dead near the living on the proving ground, the bytes counted as the relay would carry them): 8.7 KB a frame before the timer change, **6.1 KB after, about 120 KB/s to each guest** at twenty frames a second, against 950 KB/s before the wire work; a guest's upstream is about 17 KB/s (294 bytes a step). The 4 KB figure of the report is a quieter case than this one; a busy night with forty near will cost about twice this, so the next saving is the animation object itself (send its name and frame), then the interest radius. **Verified:** the sync check passes 15 of 15 with two guests, a walk, a kill, and a burning zombie; the lone-world checks and the headless hashes are unchanged. **A caution for tests:** the sync check compares a walking guest's own predicted position with the host's truth, so let the guest stop and the frames catch up before calling it.

### 2026-10-13 — The wire's pacing by a running count, and the sync check's frames marked (the coder agent's two notes)

Two notes came after the third part was built, and both were right. **A running count** paces the frames now: each step adds one over the world's time scale and a frame goes when the count reaches three, the remainder carried, never more than a frame's worth; a rule that tested the tick against three times the scale would have sent a late or a double frame when the speed switched mid-count, as someone lay down or woke. **The sync check's frames are marked** by the host: every tenth frame carries `sync`, the host hashes its world after sending it, and a guest hashes its replica after applying it, so the two sides hash the same tick wherever the frames fall; the old rule hashed only on ticks divisible by thirty on both sides, which a speed switch could push every frame off, and the check would then have silently reported nothing. The wire's close clears the count and the marks.

**Verified in two tabs on the loopback:** twenty frames per sixty steps three ticks apart; the world's scale switched to sixty for seven steps and back mid-count, the frames before and after still three ticks apart with no double and no gap; the marked frames thirty ticks apart; `check("sync")` 8 of 8 at tick 193, which is not a multiple of thirty. Alone the hash is d9a6eb31 in the pane and headless.

### 2026-10-13 — The wire, part four of four: the replica smoothed between frames

**Built.** A frame comes every third step, so a remote character would jump three steps' worth at once; instead the position a frame brings becomes the character's target, and its own position walks there over the frame's three steps, one frame behind the truth, in the character's own coordinates, so the hit tests, the name over its head, and its bubble follow the same point (the review's wish). My own player is predicted, never smoothed; a character new to the replica appears where the frame says. The sync check hashes the targets, which are the host's truth, so a replica mid-walk still agrees. The wire's close also clears the sync memory, so a stale report from an earlier wire on the same page never meets the check.

**Measured, two tabs on the loopback, the host walking on open ground:** between two frames the guest's copy of the host's player moved in three equal steps, 106.061 to 106.096 to 106.132 to 106.167, the frame's target, then on to the next target the same way; `check("sync")` agreed in every part at every reported tick.

**The wire's four parts together:** a quiet frame from 15,770 bytes sixty times a second to about 4,000 twenty times a second, roughly 80 kilobytes a second to each guest where it was 950, a sleeper's steps coalesced, the replica smooth. The first of the five items left for a friend on their own computer is done; the guest's own swing played at once is next.

### 2026-10-13 — The wire, part three of four: frames at twenty a second, paced by the world's time over its scale

**Built.** The host sends a frame on every third step at the world's normal speed, twenty a second, and on every hundred and eightieth while the world runs at sixty times with everyone asleep, which is the same fifty milliseconds as a player experiences them: a sleeper's hundred steps a browser frame coalesce into one frame instead of flooding the relay, as the review warned. The frames fall on ticks that are multiples of the cadence, so the sync check's thirtieth tick is always a frame tick. The guests' inputs come every step still and the host applies one a step, its bound untouched; the chunks touched and the pokes gather until the frame. The cadence is deterministic, paced by the loop's own steps and the world's own time scale rather than the wall clock, so the scripted two-tab tests, which step the tabs in bursts with no real time passing, stay whole.

**Measured, two tabs on the loopback:** twenty frames per sixty steps quiet and walking, 79,766 bytes a second quiet and 81,947 walking (the start of this work was about 950,000); with the world's time scale set to sixty, one frame per hundred and eighty steps, so a night's sleep costs the wire what a quiet minute does; the guest's correction stayed at zero at the new cadence; `check("sync")` 8 of 8 after a walk and a door. The fourth part smooths the replica between frames.

### 2026-10-13 — The wire, part two of four: a player's record in light and heavy parts; the rules, the carriers, and the queues when they change

**Built.** A player's record goes in two parts. The heavy part (the bag, the worn, the hands, the prop in hand, the look, the name, and the fields that never change) goes whole only when its shape changes, on a pick-up, a drop, a wear, or a lift; the light part (position to three decimals, facing, animation, hp, the needs and the combat clocks to two decimals, the action's view, the speech line) goes each frame, and only when it differs from the last sent; with nothing moved, the acknowledgement alone goes, so a guest's reconciliation still runs. A guest's HUD reads the needs and the clocks off the light record. The rules, the torch carriers, and the stations' queues go only when they change. On the guest a light record is applied in place, and the prop in hand stays while its id does. The protocol number stays 2: both parts land in one review.

**Measured, two tabs on the loopback, a quiet world of fifty-five dead:** a quiet frame 3,992 bytes (was 6,255 after part one, 15,770 before), walking and unequipping a torch 3,978 on average with one frame of 5,224 where the heavy part went; the torch reached the guest's bag; the guest's correction stayed at zero; `check("sync")` 8 of 8 after a walk, the torch, and the door. What is left in a quiet frame is the dead that move near the players, about a hundred bytes each; the third part divides it all by three.

### 2026-10-13 — The wire made to fit a home connection, part one of four: the numbers rounded, the dead as a delta with a roster, the bytes counted (docs/31/06, the owed polish; the coder agent's review of the plan built in)

**The measurement that ordered the work.** A frame from the host weighed about sixteen kilobytes, quiet or walking, sixty times a second: roughly a megabyte a second to each guest through the relay, which no home download nor Fly's metered egress should be asked for. Most of it was the fifty-five dead at a hundred bytes each every frame and the players' whole records with their bags every frame. (The host's own upload was overstated in the plan: through the relay the host sends one copy and the relay fans it out, so the weight is each guest's download and the relay's egress.)

**Built, this part.** Positions to three decimals and hp to two in every record (a position like 16.450000000000006 was eighteen characters where five would do). The dead, the villagers, and the corpses go as a delta: a record only when it differs from the last one sent, so the far dead who stand still cost nothing, and each list's roster of ids goes with the frame only when its membership changes (a spawn, a death moving a body between lists, an eviction); a character the host has not sent before goes whole, and a guest's welcome is its baseline. On the guest the records present are applied to the characters it holds, and a list's membership changes only when the frame carries its roster (the review's second point: without the roster the guest's lists would have emptied). The host drops its delta memory when the last guest leaves, so the next welcome is a baseline again. The socket's bytes are counted both ways, with the rates over the last whole second in `net.status().wire` and in the debug corner while on the wire. `net.PROTOCOL` is 2, so a stale Pages page and a new host refuse each other with the sentence for whose is newer.

**Measured, two tabs on the loopback:** a quiet frame 6,255 bytes (was 15,770), walking 6,446 (was 15,934); a staged death on the host moved the body between lists with its roster, the guest's lists followed, and `check("sync")` passed 8 of 8 before and after. What remains in a frame is the players' whole records, the rules, and the carriers every frame: the next part.

**A ruling for the third part, made now so it is on record:** the frames will be paced by the world's own time divided by its time scale (one every three steps at normal speed, one every hundred and eighty while the world runs at sixty times with everyone asleep) rather than by the wall clock the review asked for. It coalesces the sleep the same way, since a hundred and eighty steps at sixty times are fifty milliseconds as a player experiences them, and it keeps the frame cadence deterministic and the scripted two-tab tests whole, which a wall clock would not: the tabs are stepped in bursts with no real time passing.

### 2026-10-13 — Sleep in a world with friends: time skips only when everyone is in bed (Michael)

**The ruling (Michael):** in a multiplayer world sleep is possible and optional, and both players must be in bed asleep before the time jump starts. **Before:** any one player lying down set the whole world's time scale to 60 (`startSleep`), so a friend still awake had their game fast-forwarded under them, and `wake` set it back to 1 even if another was still asleep. **Now:** the time scale is the world's, computed from who is asleep (`game.timeScaleNow`): the sleep speed only while every living player in the world is asleep, ordinary time otherwise. A dormant player is out of the world's list and a dead one is skipped, so neither blocks nor forces a skip. It is evaluated each step in `game.step` (a guest who joins, leaves, wakes, or dies puts the speed right) and at the moment of lying down and waking (`player.js` takes the rule from `game.js` by `setSleepScaleRule`, so a lone player still gets the skip at once, as `check("sleep")` expects). A sleeper alone in bed while a friend is awake sleeps at ordinary speed, rested and regenerating at the sleeping rate, and is told "Time will pass faster once everyone in the world is asleep." (a lone player or the last one in bed still hears "The night goes on without you."). A sleeper is still never woken by anyone else's doing: they get up when rested and it is morning, and then the world slows for any still asleep. **Verified:** `check("sleep")` 9 of 9, three new rows with two players on one bed (one asleep and one awake: scale 1; both: 60; one wakes: back to 1); the other lone-world checks unchanged (determinism `d9a6eb31`, players 9, save 7, craft 9, wear 4, growth 9, water 8, sway 8, worlds 10), headless the same. **For the multiplayer branch:** the frame rate to a guest must be paced by real time, not by steps, because while the world is at 60 the host runs up to 120 steps per browser frame (the plan for item 1 of the bandwidth work).

### 2026-10-13 — The playtest list: a host crash of my own making, and what a joiner could not see or do

Michael's first long playtest with a friend. **The crash (mine):** `The game stopped: aimPoint(...) is not iterable`, on the host whenever the joiner drew a bow or threw a spear. The host's snapshot cleaner (the entry on the keys move) treated the aim point as an `{x, y}` object; it is a two-element array `[gx, gy]` that the bow's code unpacks, and a cleaned array became `{x: 0, y: 0}`. `cleanSnap` now keeps a point as a point and drops anything else; it is exported, and `check("players")` has a row (9 of 9). **What a joiner did not see:** (1) *anyone on fire*: the burning flag rode only in a player's whole record, and the flame particles are made by the host's step alone; the flag now rides for the dead and the villagers too, the guest runs the same particle update on its replica, and its burning lights are no longer cleared each frame. (2) *a bow or a halberd drawn in each hand*: a two-handed thing is one object in both hands on the host and came through JSON as two; the guest makes it one again by uid (it did on a character's first appearance, not on later frames). (3) *the drinking bar and a craft's bar*: a player's action holds functions and does not travel, so the joiner had the progress number and nothing to hang it on; the record now carries `actionView` (label and place) and the prompt reads it; the stations' queues ride in the frame (`queues`), so a joiner's recipes panel shows the job's time left (the chunk still carries a job's start and landing). (4) *the well's bucket*: `playedAt` rides with the sway stamps (`pokes`, now `[id, time, kind]`), and a joiner's own click on a gatherable or the well is told to the host (`poke`), which stamps it for everyone; a guest's left click now makes a gatherable sway on its own screen at all, as the host's does. **What a joiner could not do:** the items the world makes as literals had no uid and a guest's command names an item by its uid: the logs of a felled tree (`map.dropItem`) and anything pushed into a bag as it was (`grid.insert`) now get one, which is why "split into 3 planks" and the output box did nothing for a joiner (a log picked up from the ground was never in a command's reach); the bag a panel drew is also now recognised for one frame after a chunk update replaces it (`openBag.prev`), the gap in which no menu or drop found its container. **Remembered:** a world left open to friends is open again when it is played, under the same code (`open` in the world's record, set by the switch, honored by `playWorld`; if the relay still holds the old room after a refresh it asks again a few times); `check("worlds")` has two rows. **The cap:** from the front its brim was a short bar at the eye line, a monobrow; it is the dome's own lower edge across the forehead now (the side view is unchanged); the sheets differ from the last boot's for the cap alone. **Not bugs, said plainly:** E picks an item up only within about a tile (a right-click walks you there); hold E climbs a window only with its shutters open (verified: y 8.3 through to 9 with real key events), and now says "The shutters are closed." when it cannot. **A trap for tests:** a `?nosave` tab can have the title screen up over its world, and then no key reaches the world (it cost me an hour of a wrong diagnosis); hide it with `__game.screens.hide()` before testing keys. Verified in two tabs (the joiner joined by `net.open`, no `?join=`): the drink bar and prompt, both ways of the well's bucket, a craft's progress equal on both screens, a joiner's log picked up and split into three planks, the output taken by its menu, burning on the host's player and a zombie and 63 flames on the guest, a bow in both hands one object for the watcher; sync 8 of 8; lone-world checks: determinism `d9a6eb31`, players 9, save 7, craft 9, wear 4, sleep 6, growth 9, water 8, sway 8, worlds 10.

### 2026-10-13 — The real joiner's panel never sent a command: the handlers were chosen by `?join=` in the URL

**What Michael saw, after a hard refresh and a new world:** the joiner opens the dev chest, right-clicks a dev bag, Equip: the bag leaves the joiner's chest and appears neither on its back nor on the ground, the host's chest still holds it, and nothing the joiner does in a panel ever reaches the host; only after the host threw a bag on the ground could the joiner use it. **The cause** (the first answer, the uid-less garments of a body, was a real fault and is fixed, but it was not this one): `main.js` chose the panel's handlers once at boot, `createRemoteInventoryActions` if the URL had `?join=`, else the host's own `createInventoryActions`; and since the Worlds model the game honors `?join=` only in a test world (the real flow is the Worlds screen's Join and Rejoin). So **every real joiner has been on the host's handlers since then**: each panel action mutated the joiner's own copy of the world (the bag taken from its copy of the chest, the old sack dropped in its copy of the ground) and sent the host nothing; the next frame put the player back, the chest stayed wrong on the joiner's side because the host's chunk had not changed to be resent. The same condition decided the senders of Use, placement, and Make (`handlers.addressOf`), so those three were off for a real joiner too. Every two-tab test, mine and the multiplayer branch's, joined by a `?join=` URL, which is why none saw it. **Fixed (`main.js`):** the panel's handlers are one object whose every method picks the remote or the local one at the moment of the call by `net.isGuest()`; the Use, placement, and Make senders are set and cleared as the role changes (`armGuest`, checked each step). **Verified in two tabs, the joiner joined with no `?join=` in the URL (a direct `net.open`), your steps:** open the dev chest, right-click a dev bag, Wear: the joiner's own copy did not change by itself (small sack on its back, two bags in the chest); the host then wore the bag on the joiner's player, dropped the sack at its feet, left one bag in the chest; and the joiner's screen followed, the bag on its back, the sack on the ground, one bag in the chest. Lone-world checks: determinism `d9a6eb31`, players 8, save 7, craft 9, wear 4, sleep 6, growth 9, water 8, worlds 8. **A rule for the multiplayer branch and for every later test:** a joiner is a role the client has now, never a fact read once from the URL; **a two-tab test of a client feature joins by the Worlds screen's own call or by `net.open` after load, at least once, not only by `?join=`.** Not found, and so not claimed: why E seemed dead for the joiner after a failed panel action; the local handlers also ran `startAction` and the like on the joiner's copy of the player, which may be it, and the retest on this build will say.

If E stays dead with every panel shut, that is a different fault, and the next look is what the joiner's screen shows when it happens. **Also checked, and fine on the current build:** the joiner wearing a bag from the dev chest (the old sack dropped at its feet, both screens agreeing), through the relay with lag; a chest lifted and put down is findable by its id and opens for a joiner (the registry fix in the placement entry).

### 2026-10-13 — The twenty-first move: the whole-world sync check, the gate of tier 3, and the two faults it found

**The check** (`check("sync")`, `tools/checks/sync.js`; docs/31/04 "a replica check"). The whole world in parts, each hashed (`game.syncParts`): the map chunk by chunk (floors, walls, props with their bags and fires), the prop registry (every id the map answers to, as the coder agent asked after the phantom lights), the fires and whether each is lit, the players whole (position to three decimals, hands, worn, bags by uid, what is carried), the dead and the villagers as the frames carry them, the clock and the sim time and the time scale. Not in it: the generator's state and the id counter, which a replica never rolls or draws from. With reports on (`net.setSyncReports(true)` on the host, told to the guests), a guest hashes its replica after every thirtieth frame and sends the parts with the frame's tick; the host hashes its own at the same ticks and keeps the last few; the check, run on the host, compares each guest's latest report against the host's parts at that tick, part by part, naming the first that differs with both sides' counts. A hidden pane runs no frames, so a two-tab run is the script's: reports on, the tabs stepped in turn for sixty frames or more with some play in them, then the check.

**What it found, both on the host's side.** (1) **Fifteen mushrooms in the registry with no tile holding them.** The brackets (the torch posts on walls) were placed right after the map's reset and before the forage pass, and placing one grows its chunk; a chunk grown then took a fresh blank tile wherever nothing was authored, and the forage pass afterwards put its mushroom on a new authored tile that chunk never saw. The brackets are placed after the forage now; the hash is unchanged, the two-player round-trip's save a few mushrooms larger. (2) **An evicted chunk's props stayed in the registry and the fire set** (`map.evictFar` dropped the chunk alone); they leave with it now, as a guest's `installChunk` already let go of a replaced chunk's. **And one rule made:** a fire's fuel burns down every step on the host while a guest's copy refreshes only when the chunk is sent, so the host marks a fire's tile dirty at every whole unit burned (as it did at going out), a guest's hours left are never more than a unit behind, and the check compares fuel to the unit.

**A lesson for the scripted tests.** A background tab's timers are throttled by the browser, after five minutes hidden to one wake a minute, so a stepping loop that waits on `setTimeout` between steps, or a guest with `&lag=` (an inbox delay on a timer), can stall for minutes and look like a hang or a join that never lands. The two-tab scripts step in a plain loop and yield through a `MessageChannel`, which is not throttled, and use no lag; CLAUDE.md says so.

**Verified:** two tabs on the loopback, the guest joined and walking, a door opened and the torch toggled by its keys, then `check("sync")` on the host at tick 180: 8 of 8, every part agreeing, 87 chunks, 6443 props, 22 fires on both sides. Alone: the hash d9a6eb31 in the pane and headless, eviction 5 of 5, growth 9 of 9, save 7 of 7, sleep 6 of 6, the headless checks 5 of 5, the two-player round-trip 9c26c0df. **Tier 3 is whole:** every verb a guest can reach is a command, and the world agrees to the hash on both sides.

### 2026-10-13 — Placement for a guest merged, with three faults in what it stood on

The nineteenth move (placement for a guest, a prop in hand kept by the save, the climb proved) merged with no conflict; the bound and `cleanSnap` were left alone as asked, and a placement is a message, so the bound never drops one. **Found reviewing it, all in `map.js` and the guest's frame, fixed here:** (1) **a chunk delta left the props it removed in a guest's registry and fire set.** `installChunk` let go only the authored props the map source knew; a prop placed, carried, or already installed once stayed in `propById` and in `fires()` after the host removed it, so a guest saw a light burning where nothing stood (6 fires on the guest against 5 on the host with one pumpkin lifted). It now lets go every prop anchored on the chunk it replaces, and `installProp` puts the delta's back. (2) **`placeProp` did not put a prop back in the registry** (`createProp` registers, `removeProp` deletes, nothing re-registered): a prop lifted and put down could not be found by its id, so a guest's command on it (open, remove) would have found nothing. It registers again. (3) **The carried prop was rebuilt from its record on every frame** on a guest (a lifted prop is on no chunk, so the id found nothing): the ghost's turns were overwritten each frame, and a carried prop with a fire was added to the fire set afresh each time, a light left at the tile it was lifted from. A carried prop is now revived once and kept while the same id stays in hand, never in the fire set or the registry (`reviveProp(data, false)`, as `removeProp` leaves it), on the host's restore too. **Also:** the save check's in-hand row left the prop in the player's hands for every check after it (wear, sleep, water failed "Your hands are full"); it puts it back. **Verified in two tabs:** a pumpkin lifted and carried across 120 frames: the same object, 5 fires on both sides, mode `prop`; put down by command with one turn: facing left to right, on the tile, registered, 6 fires on both; removed by command: gone, registry and fires 5 on both. ### 2026-10-13 — The nineteenth move: placement for a guest, what is in hand kept by the save, the climb proved

**Placement as a command.** A guest's ghost is its own, on the replica (the carried prop's mode follows its record: `placement.syncCarried`), and its click goes to the host as a command (`placement.setSender`, `net.sendPlace`): a carried prop put down at a tile, turned as the guest turned it (the R presses counted, the host turning its copy the same number of times); a wall, door, or window built on an edge; a built thing removed, the target by address (a prop by id, a wall by its edge). The host performs it for the guest's player with the same rules its own click meets (`placement.performPlacement`), **the reach checked on the host against the guest's player** (the coder agent's rule for tier 3: a verb a guest triggers by address checks reach itself), the refusal said to the guest through the acting player; a build is the same timed action with the walk and the bar. The Build entry on a guest's menu is live. A placement is a message, not a field of the input snapshot, so the bound on a guest's inputs never drops it.

**What is in hand, kept.** A character's record carried only the id of the prop it held, and a lifted prop is on no chunk, so a save with something in hand lost it on restore, and a guest rejoining with something in hand lost it on its replica. The record now carries the prop itself (`carried`, `map.serializeProp`), and a restore, a dormant player's return, and a guest's replica revive it from the record when the id finds nothing on the map (`map.reviveProp`, a prop made from its record and placed on no tile; `game.carriedOf`). The save check has a row for it: a carryable prop lifted before the mess is in hand again after the round trip.

**Verified in two tabs on the loopback:** the guest's wall built on a free edge (free building on for the test and restored), seen on both sides, and removed by the guest by its edge, gone on both; a build two dozen tiles off refused with "Too far" in the guest's log and nothing in the host's; the guest's shutters opened by address, then **E held through the window climbed its player inside**, seen on both (the climb the eighteenth move left unproved); the dev chest lifted by the guest with its hands empty, the guest leaving and rejoining with the chest in hand on its replica, and put down by the guest's command at a tile within reach, on the map on both sides with the guest's hands empty and its mode ended; a put-down three and a half tiles off refused. ### 2026-10-13 — The keys move merged against the queue fix, and the host cleans a guest's snapshot

The eighteenth move (E, B, and T as events in a player's snapshot, performed in `game.step` for whichever player carries them) was built without the joiner's input-queue fix (the entry just below). **The two meet in `net.js`**: the conflict was the queue block; the merge keeps the bound (`MAX_BACKLOG`, ten) and carries a dropped input's `keys` onto the next as it carries the one-shot presses, so a T, an E, or a B let go with its input still lands once. Their own fix there (a held-over snapshot clears `keys`, so a tap is one step) stands, and the rule they removed (copying a press onto the last queued input) is gone in the bound as well. Verified in two tabs: one T toggles once and holds across eighty host steps; a T followed by fourteen more inputs before the host stepped (the T in an input the bound drops) toggled exactly once; the walking stall of the fix before leaves the host's copy where the joiner is, with no replay. **Added: `cleanSnap`.** The host took a guest's snapshot as sent. It now keeps the shape the step reads and nothing more: the axis to its sign, every number finite and bounded (a NaN aim would have ridden into the player's record and the save), only the six key events that exist, and `clickSpent` always false. Verified with a hostile snapshot (an axis of a billion, a NaN and a 1e300 aim, a non-number aim point, junk keys): the guest walked at normal speed, everything finite, the save without NaN. Not proved by either of us: the hold to climb in two tabs (the spawn's openings are shuttered).

### 2026-10-13 — The joiner's inputs on the host: a queue that could only fall behind (Michael's first real join)

**What he saw.** The host's walking, swings, and equipping reached the joiner at once; the joiner's walking reached the host late, sometimes minutes late, replayed "like a tape" and ending in the right place; a joiner's swing short, janky, or missing on the host's screen; a door the joiner opened showing on the host first. **The cause, reproduced in two tabs.** The host took one of a guest's inputs per step and a guest sends one per step, with no bound and no catch-up, so any burst (a covered host window that a browser slows to a crawl, a Wi-Fi stall, a relay hiccup) added a delay that never drained; over a session it only grew. The host-to-joiner direction applies every frame that has arrived in one step, so it heals itself, which is why it was perfect. The joiner's own screen looked right because its prediction replays everything unacknowledged over the host's lagging word. Reproduced: the joiner walks 120 steps while the host takes 60: the host sees it 3.6 tiles behind, and it keeps walking after the joiner stopped. **Fixed (`net.js`).** The host holds at most `MAX_BACKLOG` (10, a sixth of a second) of a guest's inputs; past that the oldest are let go and their one-shot presses (swing, alt, space) are carried onto the next so none is lost; the joiner is corrected by the frame as for any misprediction. The joiner's replay list is bounded too (`MAX_PENDING`, three seconds). The same stall now: the host's copy stands within a tenth of a tile of the joiner at once and never replays. **Two cues.** A joiner that stops getting frames for 2.5 s is told "The host's game has gone quiet. Its window may be covered or in the background."; a hosting window with friends in it says "keep this window in front" in its tab title while hidden. **Not fixed, for the multiplayer branch:** a browser slows a covered or hidden page to a crawl, and the host's world with it; the real answer is the host's simulation clock on a Worker (not throttled the same way), a move for tier 4. **A test note:** a two-tab script that steps the host more than ten steps behind the guest now loses the inputs past ten, as a real stall would; step in chunks of ten or fewer to keep a walk exact.

### 2026-10-13 — The eighteenth move: the keys for a guest (E, B, T as events in the snapshot), the interface hidden with no world, the save check's survival

**The keys.** E's tap and hold, B's tap and hold, a press of E and of T are events a client reads off its own keyboard each step (`interact.keyEvents`: the tap-or-hold trackers are the client's, with its own clock) and puts in its player's snapshot, host and guest alike; the world performs them in its step for whichever player's snapshot carries them (`game.step`, `interact.updateInteract(player, dt, keys)`): E's tap the nearest simple action, its hold the climb, B's tap a board on the nearest opening, its hold a board pulled, T the torch in or out of hand. So a guest's climb, boards, and torch are the host's doing, with its messages ("You need a plank.") finding the guest through the acting player, and the special case of a guest's E as a sent address is gone. The headless world passes no keys and hashes as before.

**Two things learned on the host's queue.** A snapshot held over while no new input arrived (the key still down) must clear its key events as it clears its presses, or a tap repeats every step (T toggled the torch out and back in); and the one-shot rule that copies a press onto the last queued input when the queue runs ahead must leave key events where they were queued, or they fire twice. A test that steps a guest twenty times before pressing queues twenty inputs on the host, which performs one a step: the event is applied when the host reaches it, not before; in play the queue stays a step deep.

**Alongside:** with no world chosen the world's interface (the bars, the belt, the log, the moodles, the clock, the names, the bubbles, the panels, the debug corner) is hidden under the black title (the coder agent's note); `check("save")` turns survival off for its run and restores it, so the hp it sets is the hp it finds in any pane (the review's "the mess survived" failing by three hundredths in a pane with survival on).

**Verified in two tabs on the loopback:** the guest's T took the torch out of its hand on the host and into its bag, and a second T brought it back, each seen on the guest; the guest's B boarded the window beside it with its own plank, the board and the plank gone seen on both sides. The hold-to-climb rides the same event and is not yet proved in two tabs (the spawn's openings are shuttered). Alone: the hash d9a6eb31 in the pane and headless, determinism, save 6 of 6 with survival restored, players 7 of 7, worlds 8 of 8, the headless checks 5 of 5; at the title the bars, the log, the belt, and the moodles are not displayed.

### 2026-10-13 — The worlds model merged (docs/31/09, moves sixteen and seventeen), and seven faults found in review

Merged from the multiplayer branch with one conflict (this log's top, both kept); checked: determinism `d9a6eb31`, headless `d9a6eb31` and `9c26c0df` (roundtrip the same), the headless checks 5 of 5, `check("worlds")` 8 of 8, players 7 of 7, craft 9 of 9, wear 4 of 4, the Worlds screen over black from the title, a host and a guest through a local relay. Fixed here, not left for the branch:

1. **The relay's address came from a header a client chooses.** The first `X-Forwarded-For` value is whatever the client sent, so every request could be a new "address" and the scan limit never bit. It is now Fly's own `Fly-Client-IP`, else the last hop a proxy appended, else the socket; the lookup table is pruned. Verified: a fixed Fly address with a different forwarding header on each of 130 requests was refused from the 121st; another address was not.
2. **The limit was too tight for its own screen.** Thirty a minute, and the Worlds screen asked for every bookmarked code every five seconds (three friends' worlds made 36). It is 120 a minute, the dot asks every ten seconds; a scan of the code space (two tree words and four characters, about 28 bits) takes years from one address.
3. **A refused or unadmitted connection still received every world frame.** The relay broadcast the host's frames to everyone in the room, so a join refused for its version, a full world, or no browser id could keep watching the world. The relay now sends a broadcast only to a guest the host has sent a `welcome`, and closes a guest the host sends a `bye`. Verified: a raw join with no browser id heard `relay`, `bye: refused`, and was closed with no frame; the real guest kept its frames.
4. **A join with no browser id made a character nobody could come back to**, one for every join, kept in the save for ever. The host refuses it (`bye: refused`).
5. **Any browser id could make a new character, without end.** The owners map and the dormant records grew with every invented id. A world remembers `MAX_OWNERS` (16) browsers; a seventeenth is told `full`, and an owner already in still gets back in. Verified on 20 joins.
6. **A sleeper's bed sets the whole world's time scale (60), and a guest who dropped in its sleep went dormant with the scale left at 60** (and the record still asleep), so the host's world ran at 60 times for good. Taking a player out of the sim now wakes it first and puts the scale back if no one else sleeps (`takePlayer`, `removePlayer`). Verified: 60 before, 1 after, the record not asleep.
7. **The browser id was `Date.now()` and five `Math.random` characters**, guessable, and it is the one proof of whose character is whose. New ids come from `crypto.getRandomValues` (16 bytes). Ids already issued stay.

Not done, for Michael: the health bars and the chat box show over the black title with no world; hiding them until a world is entered is small. `fly deploy` once more for the relay (1, 2, 3).

### 2026-10-13 — The Use command and the other player's body merged; one rule for the Use rows to come

The multiplayer branch's Use-as-a-command move (a guest's pair sent as addresses, run on the host as that player, the line said coming back through the log's sink) and the fix that draws the other player (the scene listed `S.player` alone since the first probe; it lists `S.players`) merged with no conflict and checked: players 7 of 7 on a lone world, determinism `d9a6eb31`, headless the same; two tabs on the loopback showed the host's body and name in the guest's frame, and the guest's waterskin used on its apple said "Nothing interesting happens." in the guest's log and not the host's, the mode ending at the click. **A rule for the Use rows with an `action` (none exist yet):** `performUseCommand` resolves any prop id, character id, or tile a guest names, wherever it is, so **the verb a row names checks reach itself**, as `sleep` and `craft` do ("Too far from the bed"); the command does not.

### 2026-10-13 — The seventeenth move: the rejoin (docs/31/09, its second and third steps; the review's points built in)

**The join by browser id.** A join carries the browser's id, the wire's protocol number, and the save's version, and an identity only once one was chosen. The host refuses another protocol or save shape with a word for whose is newer (`bye`, reason `version`); drops an older connection from the same browser when a newer join arrives (`replaced`), which covers a refresh with the old socket still open; and answers by the id's hash: the character this browser made here (never the host's own), back from dormancy if it went, is handed over with the welcome and no identity asked; a dead one or an unknown browser gets `who`, the guest shows the creation steps over its stand-in world, and the join goes again with the identity (`joinAs`). The host clamps what it is sent: the name's length, a listed hair style, hex colours. The welcome names the world and its host, so the guest's bookmark reads "Michael's valley". The host saves on every join and leave; a save that fails says so on screen.

**Dormancy (Michael: Zomboid's way, no more than sixty seconds).** A guest's leave, or its socket gone, starts a grace of sixty seconds of game time during which its character stands; then the character leaves the simulation, not drawn and not hunted, its record (position, bag, wounds) in the save's wrapper under the browser's hash; on the rejoin it is put back where it stood. A host leaving makes every guest's character dormant before the save. A guest's Leave world sends its leave before closing, so the grace begins at once rather than at the socket's drop.

**The store on IndexedDB** (`save.js`): the records in the large store, asynchronous, the index small in localStorage for the screen; in memory under `?nosave`; the old slot migrated the same way. **Longer codes:** two tree words and four letters (`makeCode`). **The relay** answers `GET /open?code=` with whether a host holds the code and how many are in, and rate-limits lookups and refused joins per address (thirty a minute, then no word at all), so a code cannot be scanned; redeployed as release v3. **The friends' rows carry a dot** the relay's answer colours, asked every five seconds while the Worlds screen is up; **Rejoin** with a relay polls the same answer every three seconds for a minute on a waiting screen with a Cancel and joins once the world is open, without one it joins at once. **The placeholder creation step** sits behind a constant; a host with company confirms before leaving; `&as=<name>` picks a scripted join's identity; the obsolete `identityChosen` setting is gone.

**Verified in two tabs on the loopback:** a guest joined through the Worlds screen with no identity, the host asked, the guest made one in the editor and finished the steps, the host added the character and saved the owner; the bookmark named the world and its host; the guest left, the host's grace ran and the character went dormant with its bag kept and no zombie targeting it; Rejoin from the friends' row brought the same character back at its spot with no identity asked; a second join from the same browser with the old connection still open replaced it and took the same character. With the relay on 8012: a join to a code nobody hosts refused at once with the title's sentence, the friends' row's dot reading not open, Rejoin's waiting screen, the host opening, the poll seeing it and the join going, the host asking for an identity. The relay's open query and its limit answered as written (the thirty-first request in a minute refused). ### 2026-10-13 — Ruled: worlds, identities, characters (docs/31/09), Zomboid's model, before the rest of tier 3

**What Michael found** after his first real join: it is never clear which game you are in. A world is created at load whatever you do, so New game only closes the menu; a hard refresh leaves you unsure whether you are in a new game, your old one, or need to rejoin; a rejoin gives a new survivor; hosting from the title hosts the background world and not the save; the one identity per browser is used in every game, so a name changed for one game is changed for the next; nothing says which saves exist or which were ever hosted.

**Ruling (Michael, in Zomboid's terms, which he asked for by name).** Stop conflating the character creation screen with the character. **An identity** is a name and a look, kept in the browser and reused; **a character** is a survivor in one world, owning its bag, its wounds, and later its skills and story, and **it belongs to that world, full stop**: never transported, gone with the world. Entering any world, your own or a friend's, means making a character for it from an identity; rejoining a world returns you to the character you made there, with no identity asked; a death means a new character in that same world, from any identity. The reason beyond clarity: the moment professions, skills, or story beats attach to a character, the code wants this shape, so it is built now rather than refactored later.

**With it, mine, from the walk-through of a refreshed guest** (asked and answered in the same conversation): no world runs until one is chosen on a Worlds screen that lists this browser's worlds and the friends' worlds it has joined, each with what it is and a Play, Host, Delete, or Rejoin; a world keeps its code; the host saves on every join and leave; a browser id on the join is how a world knows whose character is whose; the join carries the build's version; Rejoin keeps trying for a minute while the host is not yet up. The design, the screens, the data, the wire, and the order are `31/09`; its four questions are open (a world's name typed with a default offered; a character's look fixed after creation; a dead character's bookmark kept; spectating later), each with a recommendation. **Built before tier 3's remainder,** since it is what makes playing with a friend legible; the owed "change of name or look on a guest after join" is withdrawn, not a thing by design.

### 2026-10-13 — The relay merged and reviewed: four fixes found on the way in

The multiplayer branch's fourteenth and fifteenth moves (the three screen fixes, the relay, its deploy files, the Fly deploy) merged with no conflict. Read against the whole diff, four things were wrong and are fixed here, not left for the branch: (1) **a guest could send the host the relay's own words.** `net.receive` obeyed `{type: "hostLeft"}`, `"relay"` and `"error"` from any sender, so any guest with the code could drop the host's wire or rename the host's id. The relay's own messages carry no `from`; everything a peer sends arrives stamped with one; the three are now obeyed only without a `from`. Verified: a raw second guest sent all three, the host kept its role, its id, and its guest. (2) **A timed-out guest told the title nothing in words**: `guestStep` called `hooks.failed(code)` with no reason or role (the signature changed between the two moves), so the title said "The wire to ... is gone" instead of "No host answered ...". (3) **The code off the URL went into `innerHTML` unescaped** (the title's note, the character screen's "Who joins ...", the Host and Join screens): a crafted `?join=<img onerror=...>` link would run on the site's origin. `screens.js` has `esc()` for every code, relay address, and guest name it prints. Verified with a hostile code: shown as text, nothing runs. (4) **A guest who pressed Done on the defaults was asked again on every join**: nothing was saved, so `hasIdentity()` stayed false; Done now sets `settings.identityChosen`, which counts. Also in the relay: a socket that connects and never says who it is is let go after ten seconds (only room members were pinged, so it would have stayed open for ever). **Michael's step: `fly deploy` again to put that last one on the relay**; the client fixes need no deploy. ### 2026-10-13 — The fourteenth move: the relay (docs/31/05 item 37 in relay mode, pulled forward; docs/31/04 ruled the day before)

**Built.** `server/relay.js`: a Node process with no dependency (the WebSocket handshake and framing are its own hundred lines, RFC 6455, text frames only) that is the lobby and the pipe: the first word on a connection is `{ type: "host", code }` or `{ type: "guest", code }`; a host registers the code (a second host on it is refused, "taken"), a guest names it (none open is refused, "nohost"); from then on the host's messages go to its guests (to one when `to` names it) and a guest's to its host, `from` stamped by the relay with the connection's own id, so a guest is who the relay says it is and the host keys its guests by those ids as it keyed them by tab ids. A host gone sends its guests `hostLeft`; a guest gone sends the host the same `leave` a guest's own leave sends. Pings every thirty seconds, a connection silent ninety seconds dropped; a plain GET answers the room count (a hosting tier's health check). `node server/relay.js [port]`, or `$PORT`; the pane starts it as `relay` (`.claude/launch.json`, 8012).

**In the game,** `net.js` has two transports under one wire, the same messages on both: the BroadcastChannel loopback (the two-tab test) and the relay (a WebSocket; the guest's first join waits in a queue until the socket opens). With a relay address known (`RELAY_URL` in `net.js`, `null` until Michael deploys one, question 20 in `31/07`; or `?relay=wss://...` on the URL; `?relay=none` forces the loopback) Host a game and Join a game use it, and the Host screen says so. The wire gone from under a role reaches the client as `hooks.failed(code, why, role)` with the reason a word: `timeout` (no welcome; the wait now starts once the relay has answered the socket, since a sleeping hosting tier wakes slowly), `nohost`, `taken`, `relay` (unreachable or gone), `dropped` (a joined guest's connection lost; reconnecting is tier 4), `hostLeft`; a guest goes back to the title with the reason in words and its local world untouched, a host is told and plays on alone.

**Verified in the pane with the relay on 8012:** a host registered as c1 and a guest joined as c2 through it, the welcome (the whole save) and the frames crossing the socket; the guest walked by its test snapshot and the host saw it, with zero correction on the guest; a chat line said on the guest was on its player on both; a guest naming a code nobody hosts was back at the title at once with "No world is open under …"; the host closing its wire sent the guest to the title with "The host closed the world."; the relay's log and its status page told the same story. The loopback still joins with `?relay=none`. Alone the hash is d9a6eb31 headless.

**Owed, the deploy:** the relay has no address yet. Question 20 of `31/07` is Michael's: a free hosting tier with TLS is the recommendation; once it has a name, `RELAY_URL` carries it and the Pages site hosts and joins through it with nothing but the code. A `package.json` with a start script would let a tier find the process by itself; not added until the tier is chosen.

### 2026-10-13 — Ruled: the relay server is the transport; the GitHub Pages site is the client friends open

**What Michael found.** He hosted from the GitHub Pages site in Firefox and joined from Chrome. The host opened its world; the guest landed in a frozen world with no character screen, the dev name, and the default look. The wire today is a `BroadcastChannel`, which the browser delivers only between tabs of one origin in one browser profile, so the join reached nobody: the guest sat on the world it booted locally (a guest skips the title because its world is meant to arrive from the host) and its player, which only the host ever steps, stood still. Expected under the plan (the loopback is tier 1's transport; `31/04` names the relay for the wire), but the Host screen's code looked shareable and its small print promised less than it should have said plainly. He also had to copy the code out of the DOM: the pixel font makes some letters read alike.

**Ruling (Michael).** *The GitHub Pages site is how he tests with people online*, because a URL is far less to hand a friend than an address to paste; so the client stays static and **the transport is the relay server** of `31/04`: a small Node WebSocket process the host and every guest connect to, which forwards the host's frames to the guests and the guests' inputs and commands to the host, and which is the lobby (the host gets a code, guests type it). The same program is tier 4's dedicated server in another mode. Not chosen: WebRTC with a copy-paste handshake (the code becomes a blob of hundreds of characters each way, and some home routers need a relay anyway), WebRTC with a signalling server (two things to run instead of one).

**What follows.** (1) Three small fixes before the relay, one move: the code copyable on the Host screen (a selectable monospace field and a Copy button); a guest that gets no answer within a few seconds told so plainly with the way back to the title, never a frozen world; a guest with no saved identity sent through the character screen before it joins. (2) The relay pulled forward from tier 4, before the rest of tier 3: `tools/relay.js` (or `server/`), no npm dependency unless the hand-written handshake proves worse than `ws`; `net.js` gains a WebSocket transport beside the BroadcastChannel one, the messages unchanged (the join a load, a frame per step, inputs and commands). The Pages site is `https`, so the browser will only open `wss://`: the relay needs TLS, which a free hosting tier gives for nothing and Michael's own machine would need a tunnel for; where it runs is `31/07` question 20. The rest of tier 3 then lands over the relay, and the first real latency numbers answer the lag question of the first session with a measurement rather than a forecast. (3) `31/06` reordered accordingly; TODO's multiplayer line points here.

### 2026-10-13 — The twelfth move: a guest's panel as commands (docs/31/05 item 9, first form)

**Ruling.** A guest's inventory panel is a view on its replica, and every change it asks for is a command to the host: a drag into a slot, a drag into a grid (its own bag, the open container, or between them), a drop on the floor, a shift-click across, Rotate, Tidy, a double-click, and every entry of the item and slot menus but Use (which only begins the Use mode, whose apply is the next move's command). The command names things the way the save does: the entry by its uid, the slot by its id, the bag by an address (the player's own, a prop by id with "output" for a station's bag, a floor bag by its entry's uid on its tile, a body by id) — the same shape `containerOpened` already sends a guest. The host runs the command as the guest's player through the very handlers its own panel uses (`ui/inventory-actions.js` `performItemCommand` round `createInventoryActions`), so the rules of what fits where, what gives way, and what is refused are one code path, and a message said on the way reaches the guest through the acting player. A command on a stale view finds nothing by its uid and does nothing; a container not the player's must be within the panel's own closing distance. The guest's menus are built locally from the replica (the labels and the greyed entries are the same), with each entry's action replaced by the send.

**Two things found on the way.** The wire stamps every message's `from` with the sender, so a command's source field is `src`. A guest's replica objects are new with every frame (a full record is assigned, a chunk delta installs the prop anew), so the open container is found again by its address every frame (`interact.containerAt`), and the panel finds a drag's entry and bags again by uid and column on the drop; a drag that outlives a frame no longer holds the old objects (on the host too, where it is harmless). Separately, a host's own drag into a chest marks the chest's chunk (`handlers.changed`), so a guest with the chest open sees the change; before this a host's panel changed a container's grid and nothing told the wire.

**Verified in two tabs, 100 ms each way, the dev chest searched by the guest:** the plank moved within the guest's bag by a drag; Eat by menu took the bread; Unequip by the hand's menu put the torch in the bag; Take by menu brought the sword across, a shift-click the hatchet, a drag put the apples in the chest's free cell, a drag put the mace from the chest straight into the main hand, a drop put the plank on the floor at the guest's feet; after each the host's copy and the guest's copy listed the same entries by uid and every uid was counted once; a drop on an occupied cell was refused on the host as the panel would have refused it; two stale commands (the plank by its uid after it had left the bag, Take on the sword already taken) changed nothing; a host's transfer into the chest with its panel open reached the guest's open panel. Alone: the plain-boot hash d9a6eb31 in the pane and headless, players 6 of 6, save 6 of 6, craft 9 of 9, wear 4 of 4, water 8 of 8, sway 8 of 8, eviction 5 of 5, sleep 6 of 6, growth 9 of 9, the headless checks 5 of 5, the two-player round-trip 9c26c0df both ways.

**Owed:** the Use mode's apply as a command (a guest's click in Use mode on a panel item still resolves on its replica until then, overwritten by the next frame), the stations' panel for a guest.

### 2026-10-13 — The one-handed swing is a high-to-fall slash, never behind the back (Michael)

Michael on the first swing pass, in the frames editor: the behind-the-back part was the problem; a general one-handed swing raises to head level or a smidge higher and no more, never behind the back, and reads as a slash with a sword while still bopping plausibly with a stick; the high-to-fall slash is the play. **Ruled, and in the data:** `one_handed` is raise (pitch 150, the hand beside the head, the body back half a unit), hit on the way down (90, the body dropping a pixel and leaning in), the follow-through low and across the body (35, out 5 across), settle (55). ### 2026-10-13 — The GitHub Pages boot fixed: the character delivery is not fetched at boot, and `.nojekyll`

Michael: the Pages site always failed in the art stage on `data/art/character/layers/_superseded/hair_plaits.png`. Two causes, both fixed. The boot fetched **every** PNG in the manifest, the design agent's character delivery included, though nothing at boot draws those (the slicer's inputs and previews; the game reads the layer files installed from them): the loader now skips `data/art/character/`. And GitHub Pages runs Jekyll, which serves nothing under a folder named with a leading underscore, so `_superseded/` was a hole on the site and not on the local server: **a `.nojekyll` file at the repo's root** turns that off, so any future `_` path serves too. The lesson for deliveries: a folder the game does not draw from is data, and the boot must not depend on it.

### 2026-10-13 — On the wire the world never pauses (Michael: "never pausing is right and simplest. It's what Zomboid does"; `31/03`, `31/07` question 6 answered)

The first piece of tier 2 of the multiplayer plan, and Michael's question ("has it been accounted for that the single player hitting escape pauses the game and that can't be the case for multi?"). The plan had the rule; the code paused the loop on any screen, so a host's menu would have frozen every guest and a guest's menu would have stopped its own inputs while the host held its last one, a guest walking on through its own pause screen. **Ruled:** a screen pauses the world only in a game played alone. **On the wire, as a host or a guest, the world never pauses:** the title, the menu, the character screen, Settings, and the dead screen are this client's own over a running world; while one is up this client's player stands still (an idle snapshot goes to the world, whatever the keys say) and nothing pressed reaches the game or this client's modes; the pause screen reads "Menu: the world goes on while you are here; your character stands still." A hosting tab's world runs on its title too, so it answers joins there. **Hit-stop is refused on the wire** (`loop.setFreezeAllowed`): no one's blow holds the world for everyone; the camera's kick stays (`31/05` item 13 in its first form; the local hold of the renderer's own clock is polish owed). **Verified in two tabs:** the guest walked east on a held key, opened its menu mid-stride, and stood still through thirty steps with the key still held while frames went on arriving; the host opened its menu, the loop stayed unpaused, thirty frames went out, and the guest's player stood where it had stopped; a freeze asked for on the host was refused. A plain boot keeps its pause and its hash.

### 2026-10-13 — Grown land crosses the wire, the host's rules bind the guests, and the guest predicts its own walking (tier 1 of `31`: item 27 of `31/05`; item 35 in part; a gap Michael caught)

The seventh move of the multiplayer plan. **The gap (Michael, 2026-10-13: "does the plan take into account ownership of the settings that affect the world for everyone? Like trees or no?"):** the plan did (`31/02` section 8, `31/03`, `31/07` question 13) and the join already handed a guest the host's world whole, but a chunk the host *grew* afterwards was never sent, since growth was not a touch, and a guest could not grow it itself, because its own settings (combat testing takes every tree) could shape it differently. **Ruled by the build:** a chunk the world grows is a change the wire carries (`getChunk` names it to `takeTouched` like a mutator), and **the world's rules travel in every frame** (`rules.set` on the guest from the host's `rules`), so what the sim reads is the host's wherever it is read; the read-only settings screen for a guest (item 35) is still owed. **Prediction** (`31/04`): the guest numbers its inputs, runs `updatePlayer` on its own player at once against the replica's walls, and keeps the inputs the host has not yet acknowledged; the host queues a guest's inputs and applies one a step (holding the last when none arrives, so a held key holds), and a player's record carries the last sequence applied; on each frame the guest takes the host's record of itself and replays the unacknowledged inputs over it. `net.correction()` reads how far the last frame moved the prediction. **Verified in two tabs** with 100 ms each way: the guest's player moved 2.13 tiles during its own sixty steps before any frame came back, along the path the host then computed for it; over 120 frames the correction was zero; the host set the guest's player four chunks east and the guest's chunks went from 87 to 123 with the land real under it (156 trees within twenty tiles); a world rule flipped on the host read true on the guest. ### 2026-10-13 — The wire: a second tab walks a second player in the first tab's world (tier 1 of `31`: items 26 and 31 of `31/05` in their first form; `04`'s loopback)

The sixth move of the multiplayer plan, and the two-tab test `05-someday` named as the cheap proof. **`src/net.js`:** one host owns the world and steps it; a guest sends its input each step and receives the world, rendering it from its own player's view. The first transport is **a BroadcastChannel between two tabs of one origin** with an artificial delay each way (`?host=<code>` in one tab, `?join=<code>&lag=100` in another); what crosses it is plain data in the save's own shapes, so a WebSocket to a Node authority later carries the same. **The join is a load:** the host answers a join in its step with a new player at the spawn and the whole world as `game.serialize()`; the guest restores it, takes the player with its id as its own, and bakes the sheets. **A frame per step** (every character's record: a player's whole, the rest slim with where it is and what it is doing, a full record once for a new id; the carried lights; and **a chunk delta**: every chunk `map.touch` or `markDirty` named since the last frame, serialised whole, installed on the guest, its light forgotten and its ground re-baked), the clock, the stepped seconds, the time scale. **The guest's input** is the snapshot's walking (`axis`, `sprint`), its aim as a vector from the chest (its cursor means nothing in another view), and the shove; its clicks, keys, menus, and panels stay local and touch nothing in the world until the verbs are commands (`31/05` items 9 and 10). A guest never autosaves; the host's slot is the world's. **Verified in two tabs** with 100 ms each way, stepped from scripts because a hidden pane runs no frames: the guest joined as `P6510` with 87 chunks, 55 dead, and the host's clock; a second of walking sent from the guest moved its player on the host and the guest's own copy landed exactly where the host put it, facing and animation included, with the host's player drawn beside it; a zombie set in the guest's room on the host chased and bit the guest's player (hp 92) and not the host's, and the guest saw the chase and its own wound; a door opened on the host arrived on the guest; 270 frames. `check("wire")` reads either side's status. **Not in this tier:** prediction for the guest's own walking (it lags by the wire, which at 100 ms is a tenth of a tile of the dead's speed and a third of a tile of its own), interpolation between frames (a frame per step makes it moot over a loopback), a guest's verbs, the identity and join screens, chat. **A quirk for the checks:** the loop runs on animation frames, and a hidden pane gets none, so a two-tab test steps each tab in turn with `loop.advance` and drives the guest with `net.setTestSnapshot`.

### 2026-10-13 — More than one player in the world, before any wire (tier 1 of `31` begun: items 4, 5, 15, 18, 19, 20 of `31/05`)

The fifth move of the multiplayer plan. **The world holds a list of players** (`game.state.players`; `game.addPlayer(gx, gy)` puts another in with the kit; `__game.addPlayer` from the console) and steps each the same way in the players' order: `updatePlayer` takes its movement, sprint, and aim **from that player's snapshot** (`axis`, `sprint`, `mouse`; the player module no longer reads the keyboard: item 16's first half), `controlPlayer` runs only for a player whose client is here (the draw and the combat clock are still one per world, items 12 and 13, so an absent player's idle input must not touch them), the timed action's progress is the player's (`actionProgress`), death is per player (`gameOver { player }`, the client whose player it is shows the dead screen and writes the dead save). **The dead hunt the nearest player in sight, players preferred over villagers** (`nearestSeenPlayer`, by each player's own light and line; the chase's fallback and a spawned-aggro's first goal the nearest player alive); **a bite names its victim** (`playerHit { victim }`; the wound is the victim's, the message mine alone; `blocked` and `guardBroken` carry their player); **a carried flame is one light per player with its owner** (`light.setCarriers`; the dead lured by a torch go to where its carrier stands, which is where the source is). **"Me" is a player, not a kind**: the occlusion hole, the always-drawn exemption, and the always-shown bubble go to `player === me`, so another player is seen like anyone and never through a wall; the log names another player's line by their name. **A player's id comes from the world's counter** (`P<n>`, saved with it; never the literal `"player"`) and **a client bakes one sheet per player** (`player_<id>`, redrawn in place, placed in the atlas when first seen; the HUD's portrait and the floating bar read the player's own). The fresh-boot hash moved once more with the player's id taking a number before the dead's: `d9a6eb31` with the dev chest, the pane and `node tools/world.js --devchest` alike; two players standing (`--players=2`) `871ba946`, stable across runs and through a save round-trip. **Verified** (`check("players")`, six of six): a second player stands with their own id and sheet; seen beside me on open ground and not through my cottage's wall from outside; a zombie beside them hunts them, not me; its bite wounds them and not me; their torch at night lures a zombie to where they stand; two torches are two carried lights by owner. Determinism, growth, sleep, sway, water, eviction, craft, wear, save, the headless checks green. **Not yet:** a second keyboard or a wire (the client object and the loopback are the next move), per-player draw and combat state (tier 2), other players as things to click (loot, chat: `31/03`).
